\gamma_{2}

k_{2}
-times and jumping back to
x_{1}
. Assuming

x_{1}
and
x_{2}
close enough, the Shadowing Lemma (see
[
FH19
, Theorem 5.3.3]
) on Anosov flows produces a periodic orbit
\delta
which remains close to this almost orbit.

Fried constructed a partial section by drawing a small 4-gon
P
transverse to the flow, so that
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are two opposite corners, the two other corners are on
\delta
, and the two edges adjacent to
x_{1}
(resp.
x_{2}
) are isotopic along the flow. Take one edge adjacent to
x_{1}
and push it along the flow on the other edge. The image of the isotopy is a rectangle
R_{1}
tangent to the flow. The same procedure on
x_{2}
yield a rectangle
R_{2}
. The union
P\cup R_{1}\cup R_{2}
, illustrated in Figure
4
, is a surface partially tangent and partially transverse to the flow. It can be smoothed in a partial section bounded by
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
and
\delta
, without any consideration for the multiplicities.

Two problems arise here. If the stable and unstable leaves of
x_{i}
are not orientable, we need to travel along
x_{i}
an even amount of times to make the construction work. Additionally the multiplicity of the partial section at
\delta
can be complicated to determine in general. Using a Markov partition, we describe precisely the partial section and determine its multiplicities.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a Markov partition,
u_{1},u_{2}
be two distinct, primitive cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-words, which start with the same letter
{\mathcal{R}}
. Denote by
\gamma_{i}
the periodic orbit of
\phi
corresponding to
u_{i}
. We denote by
N_{i}\in\{1,2\}
the degree of the realization of
u_{i}
, given in
Lemma
25
.
Fix two integers
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
. We write
w=u_{1}^{k_{1}}u_{2}^{k_{2}}
and denote by
\gamma_{w}\subset M
the periodic orbit corresponding to
w
. If
\gamma_{i}
has non orientable leaves, we suppose that
k_{i}N_{i}
is even.

Lemma 32
.

Assume the stable and unstable foliations oriented. Suppose that
\overline{u_{1}}
is smaller than
\overline{u_{2}}
for both the vertical and horizontal orders. Then there exist
m\geq 1
and a partial section
S
satisfying
\partial S=(k_{1}N_{1}\gamma_{1}+k_{2}N_{2}\gamma_{2})-m\gamma_{w}
.

Suppose now that
\overline{u_{1}}<_{v}\overline{u_{2}}
and
\overline{u_{2}}<_{h}\overline{u_{1}}
. Then there exist
m\geq 1
and a partial section
S
satisfying
\partial S=-(k_{1}N_{1}\gamma_{1}+k_{2}N_{2}\gamma_{2})+m\gamma_{w}
.

If additionally
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
are both even, then
m=1
holds in the two previous statements.

We call
Fried section
the partial section
S
given in the lemma.

Figure 4:
Illustration of a 2-chain modeling a Fried Section. In green is represented a transverse polygon (it is
f(P)\subset{\mathcal{R}}
in the proof of Lemma
32
). In blue are represented two tangent rectangles. Their union can be smoothed to obtain a Fried section.

Proof.
We prove the first case, the second case is similar.
We use the notation
{\mathcal{R}}_{u}
,
\sigma^{u}
and
T^{u}
from
Section
2
.
That is
{\mathcal{R}}_{u}
is the set of elements
x\in{\mathcal{R}}
having a short itinerary along
u
,
\sigma^{u}
is the map
{\mathcal{R}}_{u}\to{\mathcal{R}}
corresponding to pushing along the flow along the itinerary
u
, and
T^{u}
is the time we push along
\phi
to go from
x
to
\sigma^{u}(x)
.

We write
v_{i}=u_{i}^{k_{i}}
. Denote by
P
the 4-gon given by
[0,1]^{2}
, equipped with the anti-clockwise orientation. By assumption, either
\gamma_{i}
has orientable leaves, or
k_{i}
is even, or
N_{i}=2
(and
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{u_{i}})
lies in the boundary of the Markov partition
\mathcal{P}
). So for
j\in\{1,2\}
,
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{v_{1}v_{2}})=\sigma^{v_{2}}(\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{v_{2}v_{1}}))
and
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{v_{2}v_{1}})=\sigma^{v_{1}}(\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{v_{1}v_{2}}))
are on the same side relatively to
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{u_{j}})
, for the horizontal and the vertical orders. In particular
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{v_{1}v_{2}})
and
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{v_{2}v_{1}})
are in the Markov sub-rectangle of
{\mathcal{R}}
which admits
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{u_{1}})
and
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{u_{2}})
as opposite corners. Therefore, we can find an immersion
f\colon P\to{\mathcal{R}}\subset M
such that:

•

(0,0)
,
(1,0)
,
(1,1)
and
(0,1)
are respectively sent to
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{u_{1}})
,
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{v_{1}v_{2}})
,
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{u_{2}})
,
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{v_{2}v_{1}})
,

•

the restrictions of
f
to each edge of
P
is transverse to the stable and unstable foliations,

•

\sigma^{v_{1}}\circ f(t,0)=f(0,t)
for every
t\in[0,1]
,

•

\sigma^{v_{2}}\circ f(t,1)=f(1,t)
for every
t\in[0,1]
.

In the
3^{rd}
point,
f(t,0)
lies in the smaller horizontal rectangle containing
f(0,0)=\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{v_{1}})
and
f(1,0)=\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{v_{1}v_{2}})
, so
f(t,0)
lies in the horizontal sub-rectangle
{\mathcal{R}}_{v_{1}}
. Therefore
\sigma^{|v_{1}|}\circ f(t,0)
is well-defined, and similarly for the
4^{th}
point.

Denote by
Q
the blow-up of
P
along its four corners, that is with each corners of
P
replaced by a quarter of a circle, and by
\pi_{Q}\colon Q\to P
be the blow-down projection. We still denote by
(t,0)
and
(0,t)
the points in
\partial Q
corresponding to lifts of
(t,0),(0,t)\in\partial P
, for
t\in(0,1)
. Denote by
(0^{+},0)
and
(0,0^{+})
the limits in
\partial Q
of
(t,0)
and
(0,t)
when
t
goes to
0
.
Take
\tau\colon Q\to\mathbb{R}
a smooth map, and consider the map
g\colon Q\to M
given by

g(x)=\phi_{\tau(x)}(f\circ\pi_{Q}(x))

We choose the map
\tau
on the edges corresponding to
[0,1]\times 0
and
0\times[0,1]
so that
\tau(t,0)=T^{v_{1}}\circ f(t,0)+\tau(0,t)
. Then we have
g(t,0)=g(0,t)
. That is the image of
g
is an immersed 8-gon, whose edges corresponding to
[0,1]\times 0
and
0\times[0,1]
coincide. Similarly we choose
\tau
on
[0,1]\times 1
and
1\times[0,1]
, so that the corresponding edges in
g(Q)
coincide. We additionally choose
\tau
so that the surface obtained by gluing the coinciding edges, is smooth. Denote by
S
that surface.

The surface
S
is constructed by pushing
\im f\subset{\mathcal{R}}
along the flow, so it is transverse to the flow in its interior. It is in fact positively transverse. Indeed we have
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{u_{1}})<_{v}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{u_{2}})
, so
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{v_{1}v_{2}})<_{v}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{v_{2}v_{1}})
. Similarly we have
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{v_{1}v_{2}})>_{h}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{v_{2}v_{1}})
. It follows that the curve
f(\partial[0,1]^{2})
is going anti-clockwise. So
f
preserves the orientation and
S
is positively transverse in its interior.

By construction,
S
has some potential boundaries only on the images by
g
of the blown-up corners of
Q
, which are sent in the orbits going through the corners of
f([0,1]^{2})
, namely
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
and
\gamma_{w}
. We need to determine the boundary of
S
.

Recall the definition of the realization of a primitive cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-word
u
. We constructed in
Section
2.1

a suspension flow
\phi^{\mathcal{P}}
for the homeomorphism
\sigma\colon\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}\to\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
, with the time function corresponding to the first-return time from
{\mathcal{R}}
to the boundary
\partial^{+}{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{R}}
of the corresponding cuboid. Let
u\colon\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}\to\mathcal{P}
be a primitive cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-word (it is not
k
periodic for
k<n
). To
u
corresponds a periodic orbit
\widetilde{\gamma}_{u}
of
\phi^{\mathcal{P}}
. The realization is the covering map, from the orbit
\widetilde{\gamma}_{u}
, to the
\phi
-periodic orbit of corresponding to
u
. Here we additionally call the length of the realization of
u
, the length of the orbit
\widetilde{\gamma}_{u}
(counted with multiplicity if
u
is a multiple of a primitive cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-word).

We have
\tau(t,0)=T^{v_{1}}\circ f(t,0)+\tau(0,t)
for
t\in(0,1)
, so at the limit we have
\tau(0^{+},0)=T^{v_{1}}\circ f(0,0)+\tau(0,0^{+})
. Therefore
T^{v_{1}}\circ f(0,0)
is the length of the boundary component of
S
corresponding to the blow-up of the corner
(0,0)
(viewed as an orbit traveled multiple times). The time
T^{v_{1}}\circ f(0,0)
is equal to
k_{1}
times the length of the realization of
u_{1}
. So the boundary component of
S
in
\gamma_{1}
has multiplicity
k_{1}N_{1}\neq 0
(recall that the realization of
u_{i}
has degree
N_{i}
). Therefore we can choose
\tau
so that
S
is additionally immersed on the boundary component corresponding to the edge
[(0,0^{+}),(0^{+},0)]\subset\partial Q
. Similarly we can choose
\tau
so that
S
is everywhere immersed, the boundary component in
\gamma_{2}
has multiplicity
k_{2}N_{2}
, and the boundary component in
\gamma_{w}
has negative multiplicity.

To be more specific, the length of the boundary of
S
in
\gamma_{w}
, is given by
-T^{v_{1}}\circ f(1,0)-T^{v_{2}}\circ f(0,1)=-T^{v_{1}v_{2}}\circ f(1,0)
. Suppose that
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
are both even. Then
w=u_{1}^{k_{1}}u_{2}^{k_{2}}
is not the power of a smaller cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-word (see
Lemma
27
).
Additionally
\gamma_{w}
is not in the boundary of
\mathcal{P}
(since it intersects the interior of the Markov rectangle
{\mathcal{R}}
in
f(1,0)
), so the realization of
\overline{w}
is of degree one. By definition of the realization, the length of the realization of
w
is
T^{w}\circ f(1,0)
, so the multiplicity of
S
in
\gamma_{w}
is -1.
∎

3.2

Birkhoff section with one boundary component

We prove Theorem
F
:
when an Anosov flow is positively skewed, it admits a Birkhoff section with only one boundary component, with multiplicity one and the corresponding periodic orbit lies in orientable stable and unstable leaves. For that, we follow the proof of Theorem

30
given in
[
ABM22
]
.

A key notion to prove the Theorem are lozenges (illustrated in Figure
5
). Denote by
({\mathcal{P}_{\phi}},\mathcal{L}^{s},\mathcal{L}^{u})
the bi-foliated plane of
\phi
. Fix a pair of orientations on
\mathcal{L}^{s}
and
\mathcal{L}^{u}
so that the orientation on
\mathcal{L}^{s}
plus the orientation on
\mathcal{L}^{u}
gives the orientation on
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
. For
x\in{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
, we denote by
\mathcal{L}^{s}_{+}(x)
the open half leaves of
x
(excluding
x
), so that the orientation goes from
x
to
\mathcal{L}^{s}_{+}(x)
. We define similarly
\mathcal{L}^{s}_{-}(x)=\mathcal{L}^{s}(x)\setminus(\mathcal{L}^{s}_{+}(x)\cup\{x\})
,
\mathcal{L}^{u}_{+}(x)
and
\mathcal{L}^{u}_{-}(x)
.

Figure 5:
Lozenge with corners
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
on the left. The two lozenges with a corner in
\xi
on the right. On the right, the flow is
\mathbb{R}
-covered and positively skewed.

For
\xi\in{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
and
\sigma,\epsilon\in\{-,+\}
, define the quadrant
Q_{\sigma\epsilon}(\xi)
as the set of points
\zeta\in{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
satisfying
\mathcal{L}^{s}_{\sigma}(\xi)\cap\mathcal{L}^{u}(\zeta)\neq\emptyset
and
\mathcal{L}^{u}_{\epsilon}(\xi)\cap\mathcal{L}^{s}(\zeta)\neq\emptyset
.
A (positive)
lozenge

L\subset{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
is a subset of the orbit space which is simultaneously the quadrant
(+,+)
of a point
\xi_{1}
and the quadrant
(-,-)
of a point
\xi_{2}
, that is
L=Q_{++}(\xi_{1})=Q_{--}(\xi_{2})
. In general, one also have to consider a second type of lozenges, defined by
Q_{+-}(\xi_{1})=Q_{-+}(\xi_{2})
, but only the first type appears in positively skewed orbit spaces. The points
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are respectively called the corners
\xi_{(-,-)}
and
\xi_{(+,+)}
of the lozenges
L
. We use lozenges later to construct positive Birkhoff sections.

When the flow is
\mathbb{R}
-covered and positively skewed, each point
\xi\in{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
bounds two positive lozenges. We denote by
L^{++}(\xi)
the lozenge for which
\xi
is a corner
\xi_{(-,-)}
, and
L^{--}(\xi)
the other lozenge.

Proof of Theorem
F
.
We adapted a proof from
[
ABM22
]
.
Denote by
\widehat{M}
the orientations-bundle covering and
\widehat{\phi}
the lifted flow. The lifted flow is also
\mathbb{R}
-covered and positively twisted, and so transitive (see Theorem
3
). Given some
\epsilon>0
, it follows from the Shadowing Lemma (see
[
FH19
, Theorem 5.3.3]
) that there exists a periodic orbit
\widehat{\gamma}
of
\widehat{\phi}
for which any point in
\widehat{M}
is a distance at most
\epsilon
from
\widehat{\gamma}
. Lift the orbit
\widehat{\gamma}
to an orbit
\widetilde{\gamma}
inside the universal covering space
\widetilde{M}
, and project it in a point
\xi\in{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
. Denote by
L
the lozenge

L^{++}(\xi)
. We take a double covering to ensure that the action
\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
preserves the orientation of the foliations on
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
. Therefore for any
g\in\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
, the lozenge
g\cdot L
is equal to
L^{++}(g\cdot\xi)
.

We can chose
\epsilon
so that every ball of radius
2\epsilon
is included in a region of
\widehat{M}
trivially bi-foliated by the weak stable and unstable foliations. It implies that any point in
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
lies in (the interior of) a lozenge of the form
g\cdot L
for some
g\in\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
. Indeed for
\zeta\in{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
, take a lift
\widetilde{x}
if
\zeta
inside
\widetilde{M}
. Then there exists a lift
g\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}
of
\gamma
which goes at distance at most
2\epsilon
from
\widetilde{x}
. The point
\zeta
and the image
g\cdot\xi
of
g\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}
in the orbit space lie in a common trivially bi-foliated region of
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
. We can additionally that
\zeta
is above and to the right of
g\cdot\xi
, which implies that
\zeta
lies in the interior of
g\cdot L
.

Let
K
be a compact subset of the orbit space
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
that satisfies

\cup_{g\in\pi_{1}(M)}g\cdot K={\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}

For example, take
K
to be the image in
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
of a big compact ball in
\widetilde{M}
. It follows from the density of periodic points that every point in
K
admits an open neighborhood lying inside a periodic lozenge (a lozenge whose corners are periodic). By compactness, there exists a finitely many
g_{i}\in\pi_{1}(\widehat{M})
which satisfy that

K\subset\cup_{i}g_{i}\cdot L

It follows

\cup_{g\in\pi_{1}(M)}g\cdot L={\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}

(2)

Denote by
\gamma
the periodic orbits in
M
corresponding to the points
\xi
(the image in
M
of the preimage of
\xi
in
\widetilde{M}
).
Also denote by
\gamma^{\prime}
the periodic orbits corresponding to the corner
\xi_{(+,+)}
of the lozenge
L
. Recall that
g_{i}\cdot L=L^{++}(g_{i}\cdot\xi)
, so the corners of the lozenge
g_{i}\cdot L
induces in
M
the same two periodic orbits
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
.

In the proof of the implication
\Leftarrow
of
[
ABM22
, Theorem A]
(end of Section 3.2), we construct a positive Birkhoff section
S_{1}
bounded by
\gamma
and by
\gamma^{\prime}
. In short, Barbot
[
Bar95b
]
constructed an immersed partial section
A
(it is like a partial section but the interior of the surface is only immersed and not embedded). The surface
A
is topologically an annulus, and is bounded by two periodic orbits
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
. It satisfies the key property that an orbit in
M
intersects the interior of
A
if and only it admits a lift in the orbit space which lies inside
L^{++}(\xi)
. Then
A
can be desingularised into an embedded partial section, which turns out to be a Birkhoff section because of this property and of the equality
2
.

Claim 1
.

If
\gamma
is
\epsilon
-dense for
\epsilon
small enough, there exists a Markov partition
\mathcal{P}
and a Markov rectangle
{\mathcal{R}}
of
\mathcal{P}
which intersects
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
in two points
p,q
which satisfy
p<_{v}q
and
q<_{h}p
, that is
p
is below and on the right of
q
.

Assume the claim and continue the proof. Denote by
n
and
m
the multiplicity of
S_{1}
along
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
. According to Lemma
32
, there exist a partial section
S_{2}
and a periodic orbit
\delta
which satisfy
\partial S_{2}=\delta-2n\gamma-2m\gamma^{\prime}
. According to Proposition
29
, there exists a Birkhoff section
S_{3}
relatively homologous to
2[S_{1}]+[S_{2}]
in
H_{2}(M,\gamma\cup\gamma^{\prime}\cup\delta,\mathbb{R})
. The boundary of
2[S_{1}]+[S_{2}]
is equal to
\delta
, with multiplicity one. Hence
S_{3}
has only one boundary component in
\delta
, with multiplicity one, so it is a positive Birkhoff section.

Notice that
\delta
is homologous to
2(n\gamma+m\gamma^{\prime})
. Recall that the map
sign\colon\pi_{1}(M)\to\{-1,1\}
is a group morphism, which measures the orientability of the stable and unstable foliations, on a neighborhood of a simple closed loop. Since
H_{1}(M,\mathbb{Z})
is the abelianization of
\pi_{1}(M)
and
\{-1,1\}
is Abelian, it factorizes into a group morphism
\widetilde{\sign}\colon H_{1}(M,\mathbb{Z})\to\{-1,1\}
. So
\widetilde{\sign}(\delta)=1
. By definition of the function
\sign
, the stable and unstable leaves of
\delta
are orientable.

Proof of Claim 1..

Ratner
[
Rat69
]
4
4

4

see
[
Iak23
, Theorem 1.3.15]
for a proof written in english.
proved the following fact for transitive Anosov flows in dimension 3. Given any periodic orbit
\delta
,
\phi
admits a Markov partition whose stable and unstable boundaries are included in the weak stable and unstable leaves of
\delta
. It follows that for any point
x
in
M
, there exists a Markov partition which contains
x
in the interior of one of its cuboid.

For all
x\in M
, take an open ball
B_{x}
centered at
x
, a Markov partition
\mathcal{P}_{x}
and a rectangle
{\mathcal{R}}_{x}
in
\mathcal{P}_{x}
(and
{\mathcal{C}}_{x}
the corresponding cuboid) for which the closure of
B_{x}
is included in the interior of
{\mathcal{C}}_{x}
. By compactness, there exists finitely many points
(x_{i})_{i}
whose corresponding open balls
B_{x_{i}}
cover the manifold.

Given
i
fixed, denote by
B^{\mathcal{R}}_{x_{i}}
the projection of
B_{x_{i}}\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{x_{i}}
onto
{\mathcal{R}}_{x_{i}}
along the backward flow. By assumption, the closure of
B^{\mathcal{R}}_{x_{i}}
is disjoint from the boundary of
{\mathcal{R}}_{x_{i}}
, so there exists an small ball
b_{i}\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{x_{i}}
which satisfies the following. For all point
p^{\prime}\in b_{i}
, its projection on
{\mathcal{R}}_{x_{i}}
is a point
p
which satisfies that for all point
q\in B^{\mathcal{R}}_{x_{i}}
, we have
p<_{v}q
and
q<_{h}p
.

Take
\epsilon
to be smaller than the radius of all the balls
b_{i}
. Given a point
q^{\prime}\in\gamma^{\prime}
, we can find
i
for which
y
belongs to
B_{x_{i}}
. Since
\gamma
is
\epsilon
-dense, it intersects the ball
b_{i}
in (at least) a point
p^{\prime}
. Then the projections
p,q
of
p^{\prime},q^{\prime}
onto
{\mathcal{R}}_{x_{i}}
are in relative positions
p<_{v}q
and
q<_{h}p
.
∎∎

4

Reeb-like property for Anosov flows

Gibbs measures are a family of invariant probability measures which satisfy properties similar to invariant smooth volume forms. On the one hand a Gibbs measure corresponds to a volume form in some other differential structure on the manifold. On the other hand Gibbs measure offer some control, which we use to obtain the Reeb-like property.

4.1

Reeb-like Gibbs measure

We introduce some thermodynamic formalism we need later, more can be found in

[
Bow08
]
.
A Hölder continuous map
\mathfrak{p}\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
is call a
Hölder potential
. Denote by
\mathfrak{P}(M)
the set of Hölder potentials on
M
, which we equip with the topology given by the sup norm
\lVert.\rVert_{\infty}
.
Denote by
h_{\mu}(\phi)
the measured entropy of
\phi
relatively to an invariant probability measure
\mu
. Given a Hölder potential
\mathfrak{p}\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
the pressure of
\mathfrak{p}
is the real number

P(\mathfrak{p})=\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{p}(\phi)}\Big(h_{\mu}(\phi)+\int_{M}\mathfrak{p}d\mu\Big)

Any invariant probability measure
\mu
for which the supremum is achieved is call a
Gibbs measure
of
\mathfrak{p}
(or the equilibrium state of
\mathfrak{p}
). It is known that for Anosov flows, Gibbs measures exist and are unique for Hölder potentials

[
Bow08
]
.
Additionally a Gibbs measure has no atom and charges every non-empty open sets.
Given a Hölder potential
\mathfrak{p}
, we denote by
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}
its Gibbs measure.

Recall that an Anosov flow is said homologically full when all homology classes in
H_{1}(M,\mathbb{Z})
can be represented by periodic orbits.

Theorem 33
(Sharp
[
Sha93
, Theorem 1]
).

An Anosov flow is homologically full if and only if it admits a null-homologous Gibbs measure.

Similarly we prove

Theorem 34
.

Any
\mathbb{R}
-covered and positively skewed Anosov flow admits a Gibbs measure satisfying the Reeb-like condition.

In order to prove the theorem, we get some control on a Gibbs measure, and use the continuity of
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}
in
\mathfrak{p}
to obtain implicitly the Gibbs measure in the theorem.

Lemma 35
.

For an Anosov flow, the maps
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathfrak{P}(M)\mapsto P(\mathfrak{p})\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathfrak{P}(M)\mapsto\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}\in\mathcal{M}_{p}(\phi)
are continuous.

Proof.
The pressure is continuous as supremum of 1-Lipschitz maps. Take
(\mathfrak{p}_{n})_{n}
a sequence of Hölder potentials converging toward a Hölder potential
\mathfrak{p}_{\infty}
. By compactness of
\mathcal{M}_{p}(\phi)
, the sequence
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}_{n}}
accumulates to a probability measure
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{p}(\phi)
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{M}\mathfrak{p}_{n}d\mu_{\mathfrak{p}_{n}}-\int_{M}\mathfrak{p}_{\infty}d\nu\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}\big|\mathfrak{p}_{n}-\mathfrak{p}_{\infty}\big|d\mu_{\mathfrak{p}_{n}}+\bigg|\int_{M}\mathfrak{p}_{\infty}d\mu_{\mathfrak{p}_{n}}-\int_{M}\mathfrak{p}_{\infty}d\nu\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\mathfrak{p}_{n}-\mathfrak{p}_{\infty}\rVert_{\infty}+\bigg|\int_{M}\mathfrak{p}_{\infty}d\mu_{\mathfrak{p}_{n}}-\int_{M}\mathfrak{p}_{\infty}d\nu\bigg|

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}0

Since the entropy
h_{\mu}(\phi)
is upper semi-continuous in
\mu
, the entropy
h_{\nu}(\phi)
is not less than the limit inf of the entropies
h_{\mu_{\mathfrak{p}_{n}}}(\phi)
. Hence we have:

\displaystyle P(\mathfrak{p}_{\infty})

\displaystyle=\liminf_{n}P(\mathfrak{p}_{n})

\displaystyle=\liminf_{n}\Big(h_{\mu_{\mathfrak{p}_{n}}}(\phi)+\int_{M}\mathfrak{p}_{n}d\mu_{\mathfrak{p}_{n}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq h_{\nu}(\phi)+\int_{M}\mathfrak{p}_{\infty}d\nu\leq P(\mathfrak{p}_{\infty})

Hence
\nu
is the Gibbs measure of
\mathfrak{p}_{\infty}
, so the map
\mathfrak{p}\mapsto\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}
is continuous.
∎

Null-homologous Gibbs measures.

We first reprove Sharp’s Theorem
33
, by constructing a null-homologous Gibbs measure. Our construction allows us to have more control on the linking numbers.
The proof goes as follows.
Lemma
38

gives a way to control the Gibbs measure. Given a Hölder potential which mostly charges one periodic orbit, its Gibbs measure charge mostly a neighborhood of that orbit. We apply this lemma on many periodic orbits, to obtain a better control on the Gibbs measure’s homology class.

Here we suppose that the flow
\phi
is homologically full.
Denote by
n
the dimension of
H_{1}(M,\mathbb{R})
. We construct a map
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\xrightarrow{}\mathfrak{P}(M)\xrightarrow{{[{\mu_{\cdot}}]_{\phi}}}H_{1}(M,\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, such that for
r
large enough,
f
has no zero on the sphere of radius
r
, and induces a map
\partial B(0,r)\to S^{n-1}
of degree one.

Let us fix some notations used in the next sections.

Notations 36
.

Fix a family of periodic orbits
\gamma_{1}^{+},\cdots,\gamma_{p}^{+}
, such that
([\gamma_{1}^{+}],\cdots,[\gamma_{p}^{+}])
is a basis of
H_{1}(M,\mathbb{R})

(see
[
PP86
]
for the existence).
According to Lemma
4
,
for each index
i
there exists a periodic orbit homologous to
-[\gamma_{i}^{+}]
, which we denote by
\gamma_{i}^{-}
.

Take two families of closed neighborhoods
U_{i}^{+}
and
U_{i}^{-}
of
\gamma_{i}^{+}
and
\gamma_{i}^{-}
, which we take pairwise disjoint. We assume that
U_{i}^{\pm}
is small, in the sense that
\cap_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\phi_{t}(U_{i}^{\pm})=\gamma_{i}^{\pm}
.

Then fix Hölder potentials
g_{i}^{+}
and
g_{i}^{-}
such that
g_{i}^{\epsilon}\equiv 1
on
\gamma_{i}^{\epsilon}
and
g_{i}^{\epsilon}\equiv 0
outside
U_{i}^{\epsilon}
, for
\epsilon\in\{+,-\}
. Define the map
\mathfrak{p}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathfrak{P}(M)
given by

\mathfrak{p}(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
x_{i}>0\end{subarray}}x_{i}\cdot g_{i}^{+}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
x_{i}<0\end{subarray}}|x_{i}|\cdot g_{i}^{-}

For each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the map
\mathfrak{p}(x)
is a Hölder potential, so it admits a Gibbs measure
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}(x)}
.
Take a basis
(\omega_{i})_{i}
of
H^{1}(M,\mathbb{R})
which is Poincaré dual to the basis
([\gamma_{i}^{+}])_{i}
. More precisely
\omega_{i}\cdot[\gamma_{j}^{+}]
equals 1 when
i=j
, and it equals 0 otherwise. We define the map

f_{\mathfrak{p}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined by

f_{\mathfrak{p}}(x)=\big({[{\mu_{\mathfrak{p}(x)}}]_{\phi}}\cdot\omega_{1},\cdots,{[{\mu_{\mathfrak{p}(x)}}]_{\phi}}\cdot\omega_{n}\big)

Proposition 37
.

There exists
r>0
such that
f_{\mathfrak{p}}^{-1}(0)\subset[-r,r]^{n}
, and such that the map
\big(x\in\partial[-r,r]^{n}\big)\mapsto\Big(\frac{f_{\mathfrak{p}}(x)}{\lVert f_{\mathfrak{p}}(x)\rVert_{2}}\in S^{n-1}\Big)
is of degree 1.
Additionally there exists
x\in(-r,r)^{n}
satisfying
f_{\mathfrak{p}}(x)=0
.

Theorem
33
is a consequence of the proposition, since
f_{\mathfrak{p}}(x)=0
if and only if
{[{\mu_{\mathfrak{p}(x)}}]_{\phi}}=0
.
To prove the proposition, we first need some control on the Gibbs measure. Denote by
h_{top}
the topological entropy and recall the variational principle:

h_{top}(\phi)=\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{p}(\phi)}h_{\mu}(\phi)

(3)

Lemma 38
.

Let
\gamma
be a periodic orbit of the flow,
D>C>0
be two real numbers,
U\subset M
be a neighborhood of
\gamma
, and
\mathfrak{p}
be a Hölder potential. Suppose that
\mathfrak{p}\leq D
with equality on
\gamma
, and
\mathfrak{p}\leq C
outside
U
. Then we have

\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}(M\setminus U)\leq\frac{h_{top}(\phi)}{D-C}

Proof.
By definition a Gibbs measure maximizes the pressure, so we have

\displaystyle D\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}(M\setminus U)+D\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}(U)

\displaystyle=D=\int_{M}\mathfrak{p}d\Leb_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}\mathfrak{p}d\Leb_{\gamma}+h_{\Leb_{\gamma}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}\mathfrak{p}d\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}+h_{\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\leq C\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}(M\setminus U)+D\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}(U)+h_{top}(\phi)

We use the variational principle (Equation
3
) for the last inequality.
It follows

(D-C)\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}(M\setminus U)\leq h_{top}(\phi)

∎

Lemma 39
.

Let
\gamma_{1}\ldots\gamma_{n}
be periodic orbits and
U_{1}\ldots U_{n}
be neighborhood of these orbits, so that for each
i
, we have
\cap_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\phi_{t}(U_{i})=\gamma_{i}
.
Let
(\mathfrak{p}_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of potentials and
D_{n}>C_{n}>0
be constants satisfying:

•

\mathfrak{p}_{n}\leq D_{n}
with equality on one of the
\gamma_{i}
,

•

\mathfrak{p}_{n}\leq C_{n}
outside
\cup_{i}U_{i}

•

D_{n}-C_{n}\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}+\infty
.

Then all the accumulation points of the sequence
(\mu_{\mathfrak{p}_{n}})_{n}
are probability measures of the form
\sum_{i}a_{i}\Leb_{\gamma_{i}}
, where
a_{i}\geq 0
.

Proof.
Let
\nu
be an accumulation point of the sequence
(\nu_{\mathfrak{p}_{n}})_{n}
. Lemma
38
implies that for each
n
, we have

\mu_{\mathfrak{p}_{n}}(M\setminus\cup_{i}U_{i})\leq\frac{h_{top}(\phi)}{D_{n}-C_{n}}

So the mass
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}_{n}}(M\setminus\cup_{i}U_{i})
of the open set
M\setminus\cup_{i}U_{i}
converges toward zero. According to the Portmanteau Lemma, we have
\nu(M\setminus\cup_{i}U_{i})=0
. Using the assumption that
U_{i}
is small, we obtain

\nu(M\setminus\cup_{i}\gamma_{i})\leq\cup_{t\in\mathbb{Q}}\nu(M\setminus\cup_{i}\phi_{t}(U_{i}))=0

Therefore
\nu
is an invariant probability measure supported on the orbits
\gamma_{i}
.
∎

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we denote by
x_{i}
its
i^{th}
coordinates, such that
x=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
. Similarly for a map
f
with values in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we write
f=(f_{1},\cdots,f_{n})
.
Denote by
\length(\gamma)
the length of a periodic orbit
\gamma
for the flow
\phi
.

Proof of Proposition
37
.
We prove that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
whose norm is large enough, there exists a linear form
l_{x}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
which satisfies that
l_{x}(x)
and
l_{x}(f_{\mathfrak{p}}(x))
are both positive. Then for all
t\in[0,1]
,
l_{x}(tx+(1-t)f_{\mathfrak{p}}(x))
is also positive. Therefore outside a large ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
t\mapsto t\id+(1-t)f_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a homotopy inside
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
. The claim follows.

Take
\epsilon_{1}\ldots\epsilon_{n}
in
\{+,-\}
and take
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
so that for all
i
, either
x_{i}=0
or
sign(x_{i})=\epsilon_{i}
. Consider the accumulation points of the measures of the form
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}(x)}
for these points
x
. According to Lemma
39
, these measures are of the form
\mu=\sum_{i}a_{i}\Leb_{\gamma_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}}
, with
a_{i}\geq 0
and
\sum_{i}a_{i}\length(\gamma_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}})=1
. So the accumulation points of
f_{\mathfrak{p}}(x)
for these points
x
are of the form
(\epsilon_{1}a_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}a_{n})
.

It follows that when
\lVert x\rVert_{\infty}
is large enough,
f_{\mathfrak{p}}(x)
is close to
(\epsilon_{1}a_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}a_{n})
.
Therefore if one write

l_{x}(y)=\sum_{i}\epsilon_{i}\length(\gamma_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}})y_{i}

the value
l_{x}(f_{\mathfrak{p}}(x))
is close to
\sum_{i}a_{i}\length(\gamma_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}})=1
, so
l_{x}(f_{\mathfrak{p}}(x))>0
when
\lVert x\rVert_{\infty}
is large enough. It is clear that
l_{x}(x)>0
, so the criterion given above applies.
∎

Gibbs measures with positive linking number.

We deform the Gibbs measure obtained in
Proposition
37

to find a Reeb-like Gibbs measure.
According to Theorem

F
,
there exists a Birkhoff section bounded by a single orbit
\delta
. The measure
\Leb_{\delta}
satisfies the Reeb-like property according to
Lemma
31
.
We extend the map
\mathfrak{p}
given in the previous subsection.
Take the sets
U_{i}^{\pm}
(from Notation
36
) disjoint from
\delta
, and take
U_{0}
to be a compact neighborhood of
\delta
, disjoints from each set
U_{i}^{\pm}
. Also take a map
g_{0}\colon M\to[0,1]
such that
g_{0}\equiv 1
on
\delta
and
g_{0}\equiv 0
outside
U_{0}
. We additionally suppose that
g_{0}<1
outside
\delta
and
g_{i}^{\pm}<1
outside
\gamma_{i}^{\pm}
.
We define the map
\mathfrak{q}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathfrak{P}(M)
by

\mathfrak{q}(x,t)=\mathfrak{p}(x)+t\cdot g_{0}(x)

Lemma 40
.

There exists a sequence
(x_{n},z_{n})_{n}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
such that
\lVert(x_{n},z_{n})\rVert_{\infty}
goes to
+\infty
, and such that for all
n
, we have
z_{n}\geq 0
and
[\mu_{\mathfrak{q}(x_{n},z_{n})}]=0
.

Lemma 41
.

For any sequence
(x_{n},z_{n})_{n}
given by the previous lemma, the sequence
\mu_{\mathfrak{q}(x_{n},z_{n})}
converges toward
\frac{\Leb_{\delta}}{\length(\delta)}
.

Proof of Lemma
40
.
Suppose that all points
(x,z)
with
x\geq 0
and with
[\mu_{\mathfrak{q}(x,z)}]=0
remain in a bounded region. Take
r>0
large, and define the maps
f_{\mathfrak{q}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
h\colon\partial[-r,r]^{n}\times[0,1]\to S^{n-1}
by

f_{\mathfrak{q}}(x,t)=({[{\mu_{\mathfrak{q}(x,t)}}]_{\phi}}\cdot\gamma_{i}^{+})_{i}

h(x,t)=\frac{f_{\mathfrak{q}}((1-t)x,rt)}{\lVert f_{\mathfrak{q}}((1-t)x,rt)\rVert_{2}}

For
r>0
large enough,
h
is well-defined, continuous and is a homotopy between a degree one map (see Lemma
37
) and a constant map. This is not possible, concluding.
∎

Proof of Lemma
41
.
Consider a sequence
(x_{n},z_{n})_{n}
as above and
\nu
an accumulation point of the sequence. According to Lemma
39
, the measure
\nu
is of the form
\nu=a_{0}\Leb_{\delta}+\sum_{i}a_{i}\Leb_{\gamma_{i}^{+}}
for some non-negative numbers
a_{i}
. Since the homology class of a measure is continuous in the measure, we have
{[{\nu}]_{\phi}}=0
. According to Lemma

12
,
we have
{[{\nu}]_{\phi}}=a_{0}[\delta]+\sum_{i}a_{i}[\gamma_{i}^{+}]
in
H_{1}(M,\mathbb{R})
. The orbit
\delta
is null-homologous and
{[{\nu}]_{\phi}}=0
, so one has
\sum_{i}a_{i}[\gamma_{i}^{+}]=0
. The family
([\gamma_{i}^{+}])_{1\leq i\leq n}
is a basis of
H_{1}(M,\mathbb{R})
, so the numbers
a_{i}
for
i\geq 1
are zero. Hence
\nu
is the unique invariant probability measure supported on
\delta
, that is
\frac{\Leb_{\delta}}{\length(\delta)}
.

The sequence
(\mu_{\mathfrak{q}(x_{n},z_{n})})_{n}
in
\mathcal{M}_{p}(\phi)
has a unique accumulating point, which is
\frac{\Leb_{\delta}}{\length(\delta)}
. Therefore it converges toward that measure.
∎

Proof of Theorem
34
.
According to Lemmas
40
and
41
, there exists a sequence of null-homologous Gibbs measure
\mu_{\mathfrak{q}(x_{n},z_{n})}
which accumulates toward
\frac{\Leb_{\delta}}{\length(\delta)}
.
According to Lemma
31
,
\Leb_{\delta}
is Reeb-like.

Recall that the linking map is continuous (which will proven in Section
5.6
). It follows from the continuity and from the compactness of the set of null-homologous invariant probability measures
(Corollary
17
),
that for
n
large enough, the measure
\mu_{\mathfrak{q}(x_{n},z_{n})}
has positive linking numbers with all null-homologous invariant probability measures. Namely
\mu_{\mathfrak{q}(x_{n},z_{n})}
has the Reeb-like property when
n
is large enough.
∎

4.2

\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
differential structure

For
0<\alpha<1
, a map is said to be of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+\alpha}
if it is of class
\mathcal{C}^{1}
and its differential is
\alpha
-Hölder continuous. A map is said to be of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
if it is of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
. A
\mathcal{C}^{1}
atlas
\mathcal{A}
on a topological manifold
M
is said to be a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
atlas if the transition maps for the atlas are all of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
. Alternatively, we say that
\mathcal{A}
induces a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
differential structure on
M
.

Fix a Hölder potential
\mathfrak{p}\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
. We construct a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
differential structure on
M
, and a reparametrization of
\phi
, which is of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
and Anosov for that differential structure. Additionally the Gibbs measure of
\phi
, associated to
\mathfrak{p}
, is induced by a Hölder volume form for the new differential structure. This has already be done by
E Cawley
[
Caw91
]

for Anosov diffeomorphisms and done by
Asaoka
[
Asa08
]

for codimension one Anosov flows.

We need an additional control on the invariant volume form, so we use the local product of Gibbs measures given by
Haydn
[
Hay94
]
, and follow Asaoka’s work.

Theorem 42
(Haydn
[
Hay94
, Theorem 2]
).

Fix a Hölder potential
\mathfrak{p}
of
\phi
. There exists positive measures
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
and
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{u}
on the strong stable and unstable foliations (that is a measure on each leaf) satisfying the properties:

1.

The measure
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}
is locally equal to a positive multiple of the product measure
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}\otimes\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{u}\otimes Leb_{\phi}
,

2.

\phi_{t}^{*}\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}=e^{-F_{t}}\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
and
\phi_{t}^{*}\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{u}=e^{F_{t}}\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{u}
, where
F_{t}(x)=\int_{0}^{t}(f\circ\phi_{s}(x)-P(f))ds
,

3.

For any points
x,x^{\prime}
such that
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}^{ss}(x)
, denote the holonomy map
h\colon\mathcal{F}^{ss}_{l}(x)\to\mathcal{F}^{ss}_{l}(x^{\prime})
from
x
to
x^{\prime}
along the weak unstable foliation, we have
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}=e^{\omega}h^{*}\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
, where
\omega
is the Hölder function given by
\omega=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\big(f\circ\phi_{t}\circ h-f\circ\phi_{t}\big)dt
. A similar statement holds for the measure
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{u}
.

4.

The measure
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
, restricted on a strong stable leaf, is finite on compact segments and positive on non-empty open subsets.

Remark 43
.

The measure
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
is a measure on each leaf of the strong stable foliation. We can also interpret it as a pseudo-transverse measure to the flow, on each weak stable leaf. That is given a point
x\in M
,
I\subset\mathcal{F}^{ss}(x)
a strong stable segment and a continuous function
T\colon I\to\mathbb{R}
, the measure
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
induces the measure
(\phi_{T})^{*}\big(e^{F_{T}}\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}\big)
on the curve
\phi_{T}(I)
.

We equip the orbit space with a family of measures on the stable and unstable foliations.
For
p\in\widetilde{M}
, we denote by
\xi_{p}
the point
\pi_{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}(p)
, and by
\theta_{p}^{s}\colon\mathcal{F}^{ss}(\widetilde{\pi}(p))\to\mathcal{L}^{s}(\xi_{p})
the lift/projection map, defined as follows. The maps
\widetilde{\pi}\colon\widetilde{M}\to M
and
\pi_{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}\colon\widetilde{M}\to{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
induce two homeomorphisms
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{ss}(p)\to\mathcal{F}^{ss}(\widetilde{\pi}(p))
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{ss}(p)\to\mathcal{L}^{s}(\pi_{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}(p))
. We define
\theta_{p}^{s}
as the composition of the corresponding homeomorphisms
\mathcal{F}^{ss}(\widetilde{\pi}(p))\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\pi}^{-1}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{ss}(p)\to\mathcal{L}^{s}(\xi_{p})
.

Then define
\mu_{p}^{s}=(\theta_{p}^{s})^{*}\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
, which is a measure on
\mathcal{L}^{s}(\xi_{p})
. The measure
\mu_{p}^{s}
induces a transverse measure to the unstable foliation, on an open subset of
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
obtained as the union of unstable leaves intersecting
\mathcal{L}^{s}(\xi_{p})
. Thanks to Theorem
42
, the transverse measure induced by
\mu_{p}^{s}
is multiplied by a Hölder continuous function when
p
varies.

Fix an orientation on
\mathcal{L}^{s}(\xi_{p})
. We denote by
\int_{a}^{b}\mu^{s}_{p}
the integral of
\mu^{s}_{p}
on the unstable segment
[a,b]\subset\mathcal{L}^{s}(\xi_{p})
, with a negative sign if the orientation on
\mathcal{L}^{s}(\xi_{p})
goes from
b
to
a
.

Lemma 44
(Asaoka
[
Asa08
, Lemma 3.5]
).

The map
\eta_{p}^{s}\colon\mathcal{L}^{s}(Q(p))\to\mathbb{R}
,
\xi\mapsto\int_{\xi_{p}}^{\xi}\mu_{p}^{s}
is a bi-Hölder homeomorphism.

Lemma 45
(Asaoka
[
Asa08
, Lemma 4.3]
).

Given a holonomy map along the weak unstable foliation
h_{p,q}\colon I\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{ss}_{l}(p)\to J\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{ss}_{l}(q)
, the map
\eta_{q}^{s}\circ h_{p,q}\circ(\eta_{p}^{s})^{-1}
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
.

The two lemmas also hold for the measures on unstable leaves. Using these lemmas, we define a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
differential structure on
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
transverse to
\mathcal{L}^{u}
. We denote by
\mathcal{A}^{\perp}_{\mathfrak{p}}
the atlas on
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
whose charts are given by the maps
(\eta_{p}^{s},\eta_{p}^{u})
.
Thanks to the two lemmas, the transition maps are of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
.
So
\mathcal{A}^{\perp}_{\mathfrak{p}}
induces a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
differential structure on the surface
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
. From the definition follow the next lemma.

Lemma 46
.

The action
\pi_{1}(M)\curvearrowright{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
for the atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
.

The Gibbs measure
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}
induces a transverse measure
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
on
M
. It lifts to a measure on
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
which is invariant by the action
\pi_{1}(M)\curvearrowright{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
. We also denote by
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
the corresponding measure on
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
.

Lemma 47
.

The measure
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
on
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
is induced by a Hölder area form on
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
, for the differential structure
\mathcal{A}^{\perp}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Proof.
It follows from Theorem
42
that
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
is locally a product of
\eta_{p}^{s}
and
\eta_{q}^{s}
, multiplied by a positive function
f
(corresponding to the functions in points 2 and 3 in the theorem). Since the foliation
\mathcal{F}^{ss}
and
\mathcal{F}^{uu}
are Hölder for the initial differential structure
(see
[
FH19
, Corollary 9.4.11]
),
the function
f
is Hölder for the initial differential structure on
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
. According to Lemma
44
, the function
f
is also Hölder for the atlas
\mathcal{A}^{\perp}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, so
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
is Hölder regular.
∎

From transverse atlas to atlas.

Given a compact surface
S\subset M
(only continuously embedded), topologically transverse to the flow, the atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
induces a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
differential structure on
S
(using a lift of
S
to
\widetilde{M}
and the projection to
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
). For that reason we view the atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
on
{\mathcal{P}_{\phi}}
as a transverse atlas to
\phi
.

Proposition 48
.

There exist a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
on
M
and a continuous reparametrization
\psi
of the flow
\phi
which satisfy the following:

•

\psi
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
for
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

•

for any
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
compact surface
S
, topologically transverse to
\psi
, the
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
differential structures on
S
induced by
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
are the same,

•

if
\id\colon(M,\phi)\to(M,\psi)
is seen as an orbit equivalence, the measure
\Theta_{\id}(\mu_{\mathfrak{p}})
is induced by a Hölder volume form for
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

We prove the proposition below.
Given a continuous and positive function
f\colon M\to\mathbb{R}^{*}_{+}
, denote by
\psi^{f}
the unique flow on
M
, differentiable in the direction of the flow (for the initial smooth structure) and for which we have
f\frac{\partial\psi^{f}_{t}}{\partial t}=\frac{\partial\phi_{t}}{\partial t}
.

We construct an atlas on
M
whose charts are given by flow boxes of the flow
\psi^{f}
for some function
f
. Given a compact surface
S\subset M
, supposed to be topologically embedded and transverse to
\phi
, and
T>0
, if the map
a\colon S\times[0,T]\to M
defined by
a(x,s)=\psi^{f}_{s}(x)
is an embedding, the image of
a
is called a flow box of
\psi^{f}
.

Fix
T>0
small and take finitely many compact surfaces
(S_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
so that the sets
\phi_{[0,T]}(S_{i})
are flow boxes. We also suppose that the union of the interior of the flow boxes
\phi_{[0,T/2]}(S_{i})
gives
M
.

Notice that for any continuous function
f\colon M\to[1,2]
, the set
\psi^{f}_{[0,T]}(S_{i})
is a flow box containing
\phi_{[0,T/2]}(S_{i})
, and is included in
\phi_{[0,T]}(S_{i})
. In particular these flow boxes cover
M
.
We denote by
a^{f}_{i}\colon S_{i}\times[0,T]\to\psi^{f}_{[0,T]}(S_{i})
the parametrization of the flow box for the flow
\psi^{f}
. That is
a^{f}_{i}(x,s)=\psi^{f}_{s}(x)
.

The map
a_{i}^{f}
induces a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
differential structure on
B_{i}
, given as the image of the product between
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
and the standard smooth structure on
[0,1]
.
Take two surfaces
S_{i},S_{j}
whose flow boxes intersect. There is a maximal set
U_{i,j}\subset S_{i}
and a continuous function
T^{f}_{i,j}\colon U_{i,j}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
, so that for any point
(x,s)\in S_{i}\times[0,1]
the point
a_{i}^{f}(x,s)
lies in
S_{j}
if and only if
x
is in
U_{i,j}
and
s=T^{f}_{i,j}(x)
. We call the map
T_{i,j}
a short return time.

Lemma 49
.

Suppose that for all
i,j\in I
, the map
T^{f}_{i,j}
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
for the atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
on
U_{i,j}\subset S_{i}
. Then all transition maps of the form
(a_{i}^{f})^{-1}\circ a_{j}^{f}
are of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
where they are well-defined. In particular the set of charts
(a_{i})_{i}
form a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
atlas on
M
, compatible with the transverse atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
.

Proof.
The map
(a_{i}^{f})^{-1}\circ a_{j}^{f}
coincides with the map
(x,s)\mapsto(\psi^{f}_{T_{i,j}(x)}(x),s+T^{f}_{i,j}(x))
. The map
(x\in U_{i,j}\subset S_{i})\mapsto(\psi^{f}_{T_{i,j}(x)}(x)\in S_{j})
is
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
, by definition of the
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
differential structure on
S_{i}
and
S_{j}
. So the map
(a_{i}^{f})^{-1}\circ a_{j}^{f}
is as regular as
T^{f}_{i,j}
.
∎

We now prove the proposition. The idea is to define locally
f
to make the short return times of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
. Given one short return time, one can build by hand
f
to make that short return time regular. To extend
f
globally, we build it piece by piece. At each step, the short return maps which are well-defined are regular. And we choose the pieces where we define
f
, so that it is afterward possible to extend it by keeping short return times regular.

Given
i,j
and a continuous function
g\colon U_{i,j}\subset S_{i}\to[1,2]
, consider the map
f
defined on
\{\phi_{t}(x),x\in U_{i,j},t\in[0,T^{1}_{i,j}(x)]\text{ or }[T^{1}_{i,j}(x),0]\}
by
f(\phi_{t}(x))=T^{1}_{i,j}(z)g(z)
. For this function
f
,
T^{f}_{i,j}
is well-defined on
U_{i,j}
and coincides with
g
, so it is as regular as
g
. To define a function
f
which guarantees the regularity of all maps
T^{f}_{i,j}
, we define
f
inductively using this idea.

Proof of Proposition
48
.
For all indexes
i<j
, up to removing a small open neighborhood of
S_{i}\cap S_{j}
to
S_{j}
, we can suppose that the surface
S_{i}
and
S_{j}
are disjoint. It can be done while preserving that the interior of the flow boxes
\phi_{(0,T/2)}(\Int S_{k})
cover
M
. Indeed the parts of the flow boxes we remove, are covered by either the flow boxes of
S_{i}
or by the interior of the flow boxes which cover
S_{i}\cap S_{j}
. Then all surfaces
S_{i}
are disjoint.

For
i,j\leq n
, we consider pairs of the form
(x,t)\in S_{i}\times\mathbb{R}
satisfying
t>0
, that
\phi_{t}(x)
lies in
S_{j}
, and that the orbit arc
\phi_{(0,t)}(x)
is disjoint from
\cup_{k}\Int S_{k}
. We say that
(x,t)
is an adjacent pair for
(i,j)
.
For
m\geq 0
, we define
A_{i,j,m}
as the set of adjacent pairs
(x,t)
for
(i,j)
, which satisfy that the curve
\phi_{(0,t)}(x)
intersects
\cup_{k}\partial S_{k}
in exactly
m
points. The surfaces
S_{k}
are called
in-between surfaces
.
We suppose that the surfaces
(S_{k})_{k}
are in general positions, so that the closures of the sets
A_{i,j,m}
are compact manifolds of dimension
2-m
. In particular
A_{i,j,m}
is finite for
m=2
, and empty for
m>2
.
Notice that every point in
M\setminus\cup_{k}\Int S_{k}
is on a unique orbit arc of the form
\phi_{(0,t)}(x)
, for an adjacent pair
(x,t)
.

To prove that all short return times are
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
, it is enough to prove that for every adjacent pair
(x,t)
for
(i,j)
, and every in-between surfaces
S_{k}
for
(x,t)
, the short return times
T^{f}_{i,j}
and
T^{f}_{i,k}
are
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
on a neighborhood of
x
. Indeed all short return times are locally sums of the short return times described above.

Now we construct
f
inductively. For each adjacent pair
(x,t)\in A_{i,j,2}
, choose a small flow box neighborhood
U\subset M
of
\phi_{[0,t](x)}
. Denote by
S_{k_{1}},S_{k_{2}}
the two in-between surfaces for the pair
(x,t)
. Choose the function
f_{U}\colon U\to[1,2]
, such that the short return times
T^{f}_{i,j}
,
T^{f}_{i,k_{1}}
and
T^{f}_{i,k_{2}}
are well-defined and of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
close to
x
.
Notice that the boundary of the 1-dimensional sub-manifold
A_{i,j,1}
is
A_{i,j,2}
. Take a small neighborhood
V
of the set:

U\cup\bigcup_{i,j}\Big(\bigcup_{(x,t)\in A_{i,j,1}}\phi_{[0,t]}(x)\Big)

Up to taking
U
and
V
smaller, we can extend
f_{U}
to a function
f_{V}\colon V\to[1,2]
satisfying the following. For every adjacent pair
(x,t)\in\cup_{i,j}A_{i,j,1}
and in-between surfaces
S_{k}
for
(x,t)
, the short return times
T^{f}_{i,j}
,
T^{f}_{i,k}
are well-defined and
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
close to
x
.
Then we can extend
f
on all
M
so that it additionally satisfies that: for every adjacent pair
(x,t)\in\cup_{i,j}A_{i,j,0}
, the short return time
T^{f}_{i,j}
is well-defined and
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
close to
x
.

Denote by
\psi=\psi^{f}
the reparametrization of
\phi
corresponding to
f
. It follows from the above discussion that the flow boxes for
\psi
, and the surfaces
S_{k}
, induce a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, compatible with the atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
. The atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is constructed using flow boxes for
\psi
, so
\psi
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
for
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. For a similar reason,
\Theta_{\id}(\mu_{\mathfrak{p}})
is Hölder regular.
∎

Lemma 50
.

There exists
T>0
for which we have
\int_{0}^{T}(\mathfrak{p}\circ\phi_{s}(x)-P(\mathfrak{p}))ds<0
for every
x\in M
.

This statement is already known for symbolic dynamic. We give a proof using only the definition of the pressure.

Proof.
Take a
\phi
-invariant probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{p}(\phi)
. By definition of the pressure, we have:

\int_{M}(\mathfrak{p}-P(\mathfrak{p}))d\mu\leq-h_{\mu}(\phi)\leq 0

The equality potential holds only when
\mu
is the Gibbs measure of
\phi
for the Hölder potential
f
, in this case we have
h_{\mu}(\phi)>0
(see
[
CFT19
, Theorem 6.1]
). Hence
\int_{M}(\mathfrak{p}-P(\mathfrak{p}))d\mu<0
holds in all cases.
Suppose that for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
x_{N}\in M
for which we have
\int_{0}^{N}(\mathfrak{p}\circ\phi_{s}(x_{N})-P(\mathfrak{p}))ds\geq 0
. Denote by
\nu_{n}
the pull back of the Lebesgue measure on
[0,T]
, by the map
t\in[0,N]\mapsto\phi_{t}(x_{N})\in M
, rescaled to be a probability measure. By compactness of the set of probability measures on
M
, the sequence
\nu_{n}
accumulates to some probability measure
\mu
. By hypothesis, we have
\int_{M}(\mathfrak{p}-P(\mathfrak{p}))d\nu_{N}\geq 0
for all
N
, so at the limit
\int_{M}(\mathfrak{p}-P(\mathfrak{p}))d\mu\geq 0
. Take a continuous function
f\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. For any
N\in\mathbb{N}
we have:

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{M}(f\circ\phi_{t}-f)d\nu_{N}\bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{N}(f\circ\phi_{t+s}(x_{N})-f\circ\phi_{t}(x_{N}))ds\bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{t}(f\circ\phi_{N+s}(x_{N})-f\circ\phi_{t}(x_{N}))ds\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2t}{N}\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}

Hence the integral
\int_{M}(f\circ\phi_{t}-f)d\nu_{N}
converges toward
\int_{M}(\mathfrak{p}\circ\phi_{t}-\mathfrak{p})d\mu=0
. It follows that
\mu
is invariant by
\phi
, which contradicts that
\int_{M}(\mathfrak{p}-P(\mathfrak{p}))d\mu
is negative. Therefore for some
T>0
, the integral
\int_{0}^{T}(\mathfrak{p}\circ\phi_{s}(x)-P(\mathfrak{p}))ds
is negative for all
x\in M
.
∎

Lemma 51
.

The flow
\psi
is Anosov for the atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Set
Y=\frac{\partial\psi_{t}}{\partial t}
. Take
m
a continuous metric on
M
, for the atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. By construction of the product atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, the foliation
\mathcal{F}^{s}
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{1}
for the atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Denote by
E^{cs}
the invariant plane field tangent to
\mathcal{F}^{s}
, for
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.
We denote by
|\det_{m}(d^{cs}\psi_{t})|
the absolute value of the determinant of the restriction of
d\psi_{t}
to
E^{cs}
, computed with the metric
m
.

Proof.
We first prove that
|\det_{m}(d^{cs}\psi_{t})|
decreases exponentially in
t\geq 0
.
Call stable curve any curve which remains in a single leaf of
\mathcal{F}^{s}
, and topologically transverse to the flow.
Denote by
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
the measure on the leaves of
\mathcal{F}^{ss}
coming from the decomposition of the measure
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}
in a local product (see Theorem
42
). As discussed previously,
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
induces a measure on every stable curve.

We define a similar measure using the metric
m
. For a point
p
and a vector
v\in T_{p}M
tangent to
\mathcal{F}^{s}
, we denote by
\lVert v\rVert^{\perp}_{m}
the minimum of
\lVert v+tY(p)\rVert_{m}
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
. For a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
stable curve
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to M
and
A\subset[0,1]
measurable, define the measure
\nu_{m}^{s}(A)
by

\nu_{m}^{s}(A)=\int_{A}\big\lVert\gamma^{\prime}(t)\big\rVert^{\perp}_{m}dt

It defines a measure
\nu_{m}^{s}
on
\mathcal{C}^{1}
stable curves.
Additionally it is of Lebesgue type. By definition of the atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, the measure
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
is also of Lebesgue type on
\mathcal{C}^{1}
stable curves. Therefore there exists a continuous function
g\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
, which satisfies
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}=e^{g}\nu_{m}^{s}
.

Lemma
50
and Theorem
42
imply that the measure
\psi_{-t}^{*}\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{s}
decreases exponentially in
t>0
, with uniform constants. By compactness,
g
is bounded. So
\psi_{-t}^{*}\nu_{m}^{s}
also decreases exponentially in
t>0
. Therefore there exists
A,B>0
for which
|\det_{m}(d^{cs}\psi_{t})|\leq Ae^{-Bt}
for all
t\geq 0
.

We use a cone argument to decompose
E^{cs}
as a sum of two invariants line bundles. Take a bi-valued continuous section
U
of
E^{cs}
, so that for every
p\in M
,
U(p)
consist of two opposites vectors not parallel to
Y(p)
. We can change the metric
m
so that
(Y,U)
gives an orthonormal basis of each plane of
E^{cs}
. Take
T>0
satisfying
Ae^{-TB}<1
. Denote by
a\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
,
b\colon M\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
two continuous maps satisfying
d\psi_{T}(U)=aY+bU
. Then we have:

|\text{det}_{m}(d^{cs}\psi_{T})|=\Bigg|\det\begin{pmatrix}1&a\\
0&b\end{pmatrix}\Bigg|=b

It follows that
b<1
. Take
\lambda>\frac{\max a}{1-\max b}
and consider the cone filed
C\subset E^{cs}
defined for
p\in M
by
C(p)=\{xU(p)+yY(p),|y|\leq\lambda|x|\}
. The image cone
\psi_{T}(C)
is the set of points of the form:

x^{\prime}b(p)U(\psi_{T}(p))+(y^{\prime}+x^{\prime}a(p))Y(\psi_{T}(p))

with
|y^{\prime}|\leq\lambda|x^{\prime}|
.
We claim that
\psi_{T}(C)
contains
C
. Indeed given
(x,y)\neq 0
with
|y|\leq\lambda|x|
, we have:

\displaystyle xU(\psi_{T}(p))+yY(\psi_{T}(p))

\displaystyle=x^{\prime}b(p)U(\psi_{T}(p))+(y^{\prime}+x^{\prime}a(p))Y(\psi_{T}(p))

\displaystyle\text{for }x^{\prime}=\frac{1}{b(p)}x

\displaystyle\text{ and }y^{\prime}=y-\frac{a(p)}{b(p)}x

Then we have:

\displaystyle\frac{|y^{\prime}|}{|x^{\prime}|}

\displaystyle=\frac{|b(p)y-a(p)x|}{|x|}

\displaystyle\leq b(p)\frac{|y|}{|x|}+a(p)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\max b+\max a<\lambda

Therefore the cone field
C
is invariant by
\psi_{-T}
for large
t>0
, and we have a uniform exponential expansion of
\psi_{-T}
on
C
, so by a classical argument of invariant cone fields, there exists a continuous invariant line bundle
E^{s}\subset C
, disjoint from the image of
Y
, which is exponentially contracted by
\psi_{t}
for
t>0
. Similarly there exists an invariant line bundle
E^{u}
which is exponentially contracted by
\psi_{t}
for
t<0
, and with
TM=E^{s}\oplus E^{u}\oplus\mathbb{R}Y
. It follows that
\psi
is Anosov.
∎

4.3

Plan of proof for Theorems
A
and
G

We give a plan for the proof of Theorem
A
: positively skewed
\mathbb{R}
-covered Anosov flows are orbit equivalent to Reeb-Anosov flows. The plan will serve as a proper proof after the development of the technical tools in the next section. The proof of Theorem
G
follows the same idea.

Plan of proof for Theorem
A
.
According to Theorems
34
,
\phi
admits a Hölder potential
\mathfrak{p}\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
whose Gibbs measure
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}
satisfies the Reeb-like condition (null-homologous and with positive linking numbers with
\mathcal{M}_{p}(\psi)
). In section
4.2
, we construct a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
on
M
and a reparametrization
\psi
of
\phi
, which is of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
and Anosov for the atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Additionally, the Gibbs measure
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
of
\psi
, corresponding to
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}
, is given by a Hölder volume form
V
for the atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

We smooth the flow with a perturbation. Recall that having positive linking numbers with
\mathcal{M}_{p}(\psi)
is an open condition among null-homologous invariant signed measures. But being null-homologous is closed and not open. So we need an extra step to obtain this property after perturbation.

We follow Asaoka’s ideas
[
Asa08
]
to smooth the flow
\psi
.
The atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
can be extended in a class
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\infty}
, for which
\psi
is generated by a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
vector field
Y
(Lemma
54
). Then we approximate the Hölder volume form
V
, corresponding to
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
, by smooth volume form
V_{n}
. The vector field
Y
can be approximated in the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
topology, be a smooth vector field
Y_{n}
which preserves
V_{n}
(see Proposition
55
, where
V
and
V_{n}
appear as
e^{\lambda}V
and
e^{\lambda_{n}}V
). Since Anosov flows are
\mathcal{C}^{1}
structurally stable, the vector field
Y_{n}
generates an Anosov flow
\psi^{n}
, orbit equivalent to
\psi
and to
\phi
. Additionally it preserves a smooth volume form
V_{n}
.

Suppose that
\iota_{Y_{n}}V_{n}
is null-homologous for
n
large enough. Since the linking number is continuous
(see Corollary
17
),
for
n
large, the probability measure induced by
V_{n}
has positive linking numbers with all null-homologous
\psi^{n}
-invariant probability measure. Hence it satisfies the Reeb-like property. It follows from
Theorem
E

that
\psi^{n}
is a reparametrization of a Reeb-Anosov flow. Hence
\phi
is orbit equivalent to a Reeb-Anosov flow.

In general,
\iota_{Y_{n}}V_{n}
is not null-cohomologous, but its cohomology class converges toward the Poincaré dual of the homology class of
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}

(see Lemmas
10
and
11
),
that is zero. We perturbate
Y_{n}
so that
\iota_{Y_{n}}V_{n}
is null-cohomologous. For that we remark that given a smooth volume form
W
, we can find smooth vector fields
Z
preserving
W
, so that
\iota_{Z}W
represent any element
\omega\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{R})
. Additionally
Z
can be taken with a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
norm bounded by the norm
\lVert\omega\rVert
multiplied by a function of
W
. The idea is to replace
Y_{n}
by
Y_{n}+Z_{n}
in the above argument, for a suitable vector field
Z_{n}
satisfying
[\iota_{Z_{n}}V_{n}]=-[\iota_{Y_{n}}V_{n}]
, and so
[\iota_{(Y_{n}+Z_{n})}V_{n}]=0
.

We would like the vector field
Y_{n}+Z_{n}
to converge toward
Y
in the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
topology. But we do not control the dependence of the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
norm of
Z_{n}
on
W=V_{n}
. For that reason, we add a step before extending the atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a smooth atlas. We fix a subset
U\subset M
which we call generating family of tori (
U
later corresponds to
\cup_{i}U_{i}
). The set
U
is taken small in some sense, so we can choose a atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\infty}
which satisfies that the measure
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
is smooth inside
U
(see Lemma
53
). We approximate the volume form
V
by smooth volume forms
V_{n}
, each of them coincides with
V
inside
U
. And as above, we approximate
Y
by a sequence of vector fields
Y_{n}
, so that
Y_{n}
preserves
V_{n}
.

We choose the set
U
so that the map
H^{2}(U,\mathbb{R})\to H^{2}(M,\mathbb{R})
is surjective. Then we can take a vector field
Z_{n}
as above, but supported inside
U
(see Lemma
52
). In particular
Z_{n}
is chosen relatively to a fixed volume form
(V_{n})_{|U}=V_{|U}
. Therefore its
\mathcal{C}^{1}
norm is smaller than a constant (which depends on the constant
V_{|U}
only) multiplied by the norm of
[\iota_{Y_{n}}V_{n}]
, which converges toward zero. Then
Y_{n}+Z_{n}
converges toward
Y
and
\iota_{(Y_{n}+Z_{n})}V_{n}
is null-homologous as previously. It follows that for
n
large enough,
Y_{n}+Z_{n}
generates a reparameterized smooth Reeb-Anosov flow which is orbit equivalent to
\phi
.
∎

In the proof, it is important to start with a measure which has a positive linking number with all
\mathcal{M}^{0}_{p}(\phi)
(a Reeb-like measure), and to carry that property until we can apply McDuff criterion, which itself requires a positive linking number property.

The proof of Theorem
G
follows the same plan, without the consideration for the linking number.

4.4

Smoothing the flow

Given a class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
Anosov flow preserving a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
volume form, we smooth them with an adaptation of Asaoka’s technique.

In this section
\phi
is supposed homologically full,
\mathfrak{p}
is a Hölder potential so that
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}
is null-homologous,
\psi
is a reparametrization of
\phi
, of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
and Anosov for the atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. We also denote by
Y=\frac{\partial\psi_{t}}{\partial t}
the vector field generator of
\psi
, and
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}=cste\cdot\Theta_{\id}(\mu_{\mathfrak{p}})
the
\psi
-invariant probability corresponding to
\mu_{\mathfrak{p}}
. The measure
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
is null-homologous and is induced by a Hölder volume form. When
\phi
is additionally
\mathbb{R}
-covered and positively skewed, we additionally suppose that
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
satisfies the Reeb-like condition.

Denote by
D^{2}
the unit disc in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. A
generating family of tori
is a finite family of disjoint compact subsets
(U_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
of
M
, such that there exists some homeomorphisms
f_{i}\colon D^{2}\times S^{1}\to U_{i}
, satisfying that the homology classes of the curves
f_{i}(0\times S^{1})
form a basis of
H_{1}(M,\mathbb{R})
. When
M
is a
\mathbb{R}
-homology sphere, generating families of tori are empty families by convention.
Given a generating family of tori
(U_{i})_{i}
, a generating sub-family of tori is a generating family of tori
(V_{i})_{i}
with
V_{i}\subset U_{i}
for all
i
.

The purpure of generating families of tori is to have a subset
U\subset M
for which
H_{1}(U,\mathbb{R})\to H_{1}(M,\mathbb{R})
is surjective, but which is small enough so that we can extend the
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
structure
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
on
M
, into a smooth structure for which the flow and the invariant volume form are smooth inside
U
.
Denote by
\Lambda^{\infty}(T_{M})
the set of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
vector fields on
M
. Take a norm
\lVert.\rVert
on
H_{1}(M,\mathbb{R})
.

Lemma 52
.

Let
M
be a smooth closed 3-manifold with a generating family of tori
(U_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
. Fix a volume form
V
on
M
, supposed to be smooth on each
U_{i}
. Then there exist
A>0
and a continuous map
v\colon H^{2}(M,\mathbb{R})\to\Lambda^{\infty}(T_{M})
, such that for each
x\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{R})
, the following holds:

•

v(x)\equiv 0
outside
\cup_{i}U_{i}
,

•

v(x)
preserves
V
, that is
\mathcal{L}_{v(x)}V=0
,

•

\lVert v(x)\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}}\leq A\lVert x\rVert
,

•

[\iota_{v(x)}V]=x
in
H^{2}(M,\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.
We prove a similar result for the manifold
N=D^{2}\times S^{1}
, and push it forward to
M
.
We denote by
(x,y)
the coordinates on
D^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, by
\theta
the coordinate on
S^{1}
, and
V_{0}=dx\wedge dy\wedge d\theta
.
Take a function
h\colon D^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
h\equiv 0
near
\partial D^{2}
and
\int_{D^{2}}h(x,y)dx\wedge dy=1
. We define on
N
the vector field
Y=h(x,y)\partial_{\theta}
.

One has
\mathcal{L}_{Y}(V_{0})=d(\iota_{Y}V_{0})=d(h(x,y)dx\wedge dy)=0
, so the vector field
Y
preserves the volume form
V_{0}
. Let
S\subset N
be an oriented compact surface whose boundary is inside
\partial N
. We claim that the integral
\int_{S}\iota_{Y}V_{0}
is equal to the algebraic intersection
S\mathrlap{\hskip 2.3pt\cdot}{\cap}(0\times S^{1})
. To prove that, notice that
\iota_{X}V_{0}
is closed and equal to zero close to
\partial N
, so
\int_{S}\iota_{X}V_{0}
depends only on the homology class of
S
relatively to
\partial N
. The equality
\int_{S}\iota_{X}V_{0}=S\mathrlap{\hskip 2.3pt\cdot}{\cap}(0\times S^{1})
holds when
S=D^{2}\times\{*\}
, so it holds for any
S
by linearity.

Up to taking a generating sub-family of tori of
(U_{i})
, we can suppose that there exist some smooth parametrizations
f_{i}\colon N\to U_{i}
.
Applying Moser’s lemma, we can take
f_{i}
such that
f_{i}^{*}V=\lambda_{i}V_{0}
for some
\lambda_{i}>0
.
Denote
Y_{i}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}}df_{i}(Y)
, which we extend to a smooth vector field on
M
by setting
Y_{i}\equiv 0
outside
U_{i}
. We have
\mathcal{L}_{Y_{i}}V=0
. Additionally for any closed surface
S\subset M
, the integral
\int_{S}\iota_{Y_{i}}V
is equal to
S\mathrlap{\hskip 2.3pt\cdot}{\cap}\gamma_{i}
, where
\gamma_{i}
is the closed curve corresponding to
f_{i}(0\times S^{1})
. Therefore
[\iota_{Y_{i}}V]\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{R})
is Poincaré-dual to
[\gamma_{i}]\in H_{1}(M,\mathbb{R})
.

For any
x\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{R})
, we write
x=\sum_{i}x_{i}[\gamma_{i}]
and define
v(x)=\sum_{i}x_{i}Y_{i}
. Then we have
[\iota_{v(x)}V]=x
and
\lVert v(x)\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}}\leq C\lVert x\rVert\max_{i}\lVert Y_{i}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{2}}
for some
C>0
.
∎

We denote by
f^{*}Y
the vector field
x\mapsto df(f^{-1}(x))(Y\circ f^{-1}(x))
, conjugated to
Y
by a diffeomorphism
f
.

Lemma 53
.

There exists a generating family of tori
(U_{i})_{i}
and an embedding
f\colon\cup_{i}U_{i}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
for the atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, such that

•

f^{*}\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
f^{*}Y
are smooth measure on each
U_{i}
,

•

f(V_{i})
is smooth.

Proof.
First construct a generating family of tori by taking a family of closed
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
curves
\gamma_{i}
whose homology classes form a basis of
H_{1}(M,\mathbb{R})
.
These curves can be taken embedded and disjoint. Then fix a family of disjoint compact neighborhoods
(U_{i})_{i}
of the curves
\gamma_{i}
.

Inside
U_{i}
, thicken
\gamma_{i}
in a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
compact annulus
A_{i}
, transverse to the flow
\psi
. We take a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
embedding
g_{i}\colon A_{i}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The measure
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
is given by a Hölder volume form
V
on
M
, and
Y
is Hölder, so
i(Y)V
is a Hölder differential 2-form on
A_{i}
. Since
A_{i}
is transverse to
Y
,
i(Y)V
is additionally an area form on
A_{i}
. Hence
(g_{i}^{-1})^{*}(i(Y)V)
is a Hölder area form on
g_{i}(A_{i})
.

We can suppose that
A_{i}
has been taken such that
g_{i}(A_{i})
has a smooth boundary in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
In
[
DM90
, Theorem 1]

is proven a generalization of Moser’s Lemma, which state that there exists a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
diffeomorphism
h_{i}\colon g_{i}(A_{i})\to g_{i}(A_{i})
such that
(h_{i}\circ g_{i})^{*}\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
is given by a smooth volume form on
g_{i}(V_{i})
. Now define the map
f_{i}\colon\psi_{[0,\epsilon]}(A_{i})\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
by
f_{i}(\psi_{t}(x))=(h\circ g(x),t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
. Since
\psi
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
,
f_{i}
is also of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
. By construction
df(Y)=\partial_{z}
is constant. Take a closed simple curve
\delta_{i}
in
\psi_{[0,\epsilon]}(A_{i})
homotopic to
\gamma_{i}
inside
U_{i}
such that
f(\delta_{i})
is smooth, and take a neighborhood
V_{i}
of
\delta_{i}
inside
\psi_{[0,\epsilon]}(A_{i})
, such that
f_{i}(V_{i})
is smooth. The map
f
defined as
f_{|V_{i}}\equiv f_{i}+c_{i}
for some constants
c_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfies the lemma.
∎

Regularity of the generator.

The flow
\psi
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
. We improve the regularity of
\psi
in two steps. First we find a smooth structure for which the vector field
Y
generating
\phi
is also of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
. Then we perturbate
Y
into a smooth Anosov flow. We need additional considerations when we perturbate
Y
, so that the resulting flow preserves a smooth volume with the Reeb property. We use a generating sub-family of tori to improve the regularity of the Gibbs measure on an open subset. It improves our control when doing the last perturbation.

Lemma 54
.

There exists a generating sub-family of tori
U_{i}
and a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
smooth structure
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\infty}
on
M
which extends the
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
structure
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, and such that for the atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\infty}
, we have:

•

Y
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
for
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\infty}
,

•

\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
is smooth inside each
U_{i}
.

Proof of Lemma
54
.
Let
(U_{i})_{i}
be a generating family of tori. Take a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
map
f\colon\cup_{i}U_{i}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
given by Lemma
53
, so that
f^{*}\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
is smooth. Take
n\geq 2
and consider a
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
map
g\colon M\to\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}
which extends
f\times 0
on
\cup_{i}U_{i}
. If
g
is chosen generic outside each
U_{i}
, then it is an embedding. Up to doing a
\mathcal{C}^{1+\alpha}
isotopy of
g
, we can suppose that the image of
g
is a smooth sub-manifold of
\mathbb{R}^{2n+3}
. Then the smooth structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2n+3}
pushes back to a smooth structure on
M
which extends the
\mathcal{C}^{1+H}
atlas
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\infty}
.

Take
\alpha\in(0,1)
so that
\psi
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+\alpha}
for the new smooth structure. Hart proved

[
Har83
, Theorem A]

that there exists a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
diffeomorphism
h\colon M\to M
which conjugates
\psi
with a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
flow whose generating vector field is also
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. Replacing
\mathcal{C}^{k}
by
\mathcal{C}^{k+\alpha}
in Hart’s proof yields a
\mathcal{C}^{1+\alpha}
diffeomorphism
h\colon M\to M
which conjugates
Y
with a
\mathcal{C}^{1+\alpha}
vector field, so that
h
is the identity on
\cup_{i}U_{i}
. An adaptation of Hart’s proof to the case
\mathcal{C}^{k+\alpha}
is provided
in Appendix
C
.
Then
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
is smooth for the new smooth structure since
h
coincides with the identity inside
\cup_{i}U_{i}
.
∎

Smoothing the generator.

For this subsection, we use the notation from Lemma
54
and consider the atlas denoted by
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\infty}
. According to the lemma, there exists a generating family of tori
(U_{i})_{i}
, on which
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
is smooth. Take
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that
Y
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{1+\alpha}
and such that
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
is induced by an
\alpha
-Hölder volume form on
M
. Let
V
be a smooth volume form on
M
and
\lambda\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
be the function which satisfies
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}=e^{\lambda}V
. It is
\alpha
-Hölder continuous on
M
and smooth on each tori
U_{i}
. Take
\lambda_{k}\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
a sequence of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
function, which approximates
\lambda
for the
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}
topology, and which coincide with
\lambda
on each
U_{i}
.

Proposition 55
(Asaoka
[
Asa08
, Proposition 2.3]
).

There exists a sequence of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
vector fields
Y_{k}
on
M
such that:

•

(Y_{k})_{k}
converges toward
Y
in the
\mathcal{C}^{1+\alpha}
topology,

•

Y_{k}
preserves
e^{\lambda_{k}}V
for all
k
,

Asaoka state a weaker version for this proposition. But he proves the version stated above.

Denote by
\psi^{k}
the flow generated by
Y_{k}
. By
\mathcal{C}^{1}
structural stability of Anosov flows,
\psi^{k}
is Anosov and orbit equivalent to
\phi
for
k
large enough
(see
[
KKW91
, Proposition 3]
).
Denote by
h_{n}\colon(M,\psi^{k})\to(M,\psi)
an orbit equivalence so that
(h_{n})_{n}
converges toward
\id
for the
\mathcal{C}^{0}
topology. Also denote by
\mu_{k}
the probability measure induced by
e^{\lambda_{k}}V
, invariant by
\psi^{k}
.

Lemma 56
.

The sequence of measures
\Theta_{h_{n}}(\mu_{n})\in\mathcal{M}_{p}(\psi)
converges toward
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Proof.
We prove that the sequence of transverse measures
h_{k}^{*}(\mu_{k}^{\perp})
converges toward
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
, which implies the lemma. Recall that
(\lambda_{k})_{k}
converges toward
\lambda
, so
(\mu_{k})_{k}
converges toward
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Let
S\subset M
be a compact surface, transverse to
Y
and
Y_{k}
for all
k
. So there exists
\epsilon>0
for which the maps
\psi^{k}
are all injective on the set
[0,\epsilon]\times S\to M
. Then for any
k
, we have
\mu_{k}^{\perp}(S)=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mu_{k}(\psi^{k}_{[0,\epsilon]}(S))\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}
.

Take a continuous function
f\colon S\to\mathbb{R}
so that
f\equiv 0
on a neighborhood of
\partial S
. We can extend
f
to a continuous function
M\to\mathbb{R}
, such that
f\equiv 0
on a neighborhood of
\partial S
inside
M
. Fix three neighborhoods
U_{1},U_{2},U_{3}\subset S
of
\partial S
for which
f_{U_{i}}=0
and
\overline{U}_{i}\subset\Int U_{i+1}
.

Since the sequence
(h_{k})_{k}
converges toward the identity, there exists for
k
large enough an embedding
g_{k}\colon h_{k}^{-1}(S\setminus U_{1})\to S
, obtained as a small isotopy along the flow
\psi^{k}
from
h_{k}^{-1}(S\setminus U_{1})
to
S
. For
k
large enough, the image of
g_{k}
contains
U_{2}
. Since
h_{k}
converges toward
\id
and
Y_{k}
converges toward
Y
, the map
g_{k}\circ h_{k}^{-1}
converges toward the inclusion
S\setminus U_{1}\hookrightarrow S
. Hence for
k
large we have
S\setminus U_{2}\subset g_{k}\circ h_{k}^{-1}(S\setminus U_{1})
. Therefore for large
k
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\int_{S}fdh_{k}^{*}(\mu_{k}^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\int_{S\setminus U_{1}}fdh_{k}^{*}(\mu_{k}^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\int_{h_{k}^{-1}(S\setminus U_{1})}f\circ h_{k}d\mu_{k}^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\int_{g_{k}\circ h_{k}^{-1}(S\setminus U_{1})}f\circ h_{k}\circ g_{k}^{-1}d\mu_{k}^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\int_{S\setminus U_{2}}f\circ h_{k}\circ g_{k}^{-1}d\mu_{k}^{\perp}

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{S}fd\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}-\int_{S}fdh_{k}^{*}(\mu_{k}^{\perp})\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg|\int_{S}fd\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}-\int_{S\setminus U_{2}}f\circ h_{k}\circ g_{k}^{-1}d\mu_{k}^{\perp}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg|\int_{S\setminus U_{2}}fd\big(\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}-\mu_{k}^{\perp}\big)\bigg|+\int_{S\setminus U_{2}}\big|f-f\circ h_{k}\circ g_{k}^{-1}\big|d\mu_{k}^{\perp}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg|\int_{S\setminus U_{2}}fd\big(\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}-\mu_{k}^{\perp}\big)\bigg|+\mu_{k}^{\perp}(S)\lVert f-f\circ h_{k}\circ g_{k}^{-1}\rVert_{S\setminus U_{2}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{}0

The first term converges toward zero since
\mu_{k}
converges toward
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
. The second term converges toward zero since we have
\mu_{k}^{\perp}(S)\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}
, and
(f\circ h_{k}\circ g_{k}^{-1})_{k}
converges toward
f
on
S\setminus U_{2}
. Therefore the measure
h_{k}^{*}(\mu_{k}^{\perp})
converges toward
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\perp}
.
∎

Lemma 57
.

There exists a sequence
Z_{k}
of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
vector fields on
M
such that:

•

Z_{k}
converges toward zero in the
\mathcal{C}^{1+\alpha}
topology,

•

Z_{k}
preserves
e^{\lambda_{k}}V
for all
k
,

•

\iota_{Y_{k}}e^{\lambda_{k}}V
and
\iota_{Z_{k}}e^{\lambda_{k}}V
are cohomologous.

Proof.
Let
v\colon H^{2}(M,\mathbb{R})\to\Lambda^{\infty}(TM)
be the map constructed in Lemma
52
for the volume form
e^{\lambda}V
, which is smooth inside each
U_{i}
. Notice that for
x\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{R})
,
v(x)
is a vector field supported inside the union of the tori
U_{i}
, where
\lambda
and
\lambda_{k}
coincide. Hence the vector field
v(x)
preserves the volume forms
e^{\lambda_{k}}V
. Now take
Z_{k}=v([\iota_{Y_{k}}e^{\lambda_{k}}V])
. By hypothesis, we have
[\iota_{Z_{k}}e^{\lambda_{k}}V]=[\iota_{Y_{k}}e^{\lambda_{k}}V]
, which converges toward zero according to
Lemmas
56
and
11
.
It follows that
Z_{k}
converges toward zero for the
\mathcal{C}^{2}
topology, and so also for the
\mathcal{C}^{1+\alpha}
topology.
∎

Corollary 58
.

For
k
large enough, the smooth vector field
Y_{k}-Z_{k}
generates an Anosov flow, orbit equivalent to
\phi
, which preserves the smooth volume form
e^{\lambda_{k}}V
. Additionally the probability measure induced by
e^{\lambda_{k}}V
satisfies the Reeb-like property for
k
large enough.

The corollary and Theorem
E
give a proof to Theorem
A
.

Proof.
Denote by
\psi^{n}
the flow generated by the vector field
Y_{n}-Z_{n}
.
The vector fields
(Y_{k}-Z_{k})
converges toward
Y
for the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
topology. So by structural stability of Anosov flows

[
KKW91
, Proposition 3]
,
for
k
large enough,
\psi^{n}
is Anosov and is orbit equivalent to
\phi
. Denote by
\eta_{n}
the
\psi^{n}
-invariant probability measure induced by
\iota_{(Y_{k}-Z_{k})}e^{\lambda_{k}}V
. It follows from the previous lemma that
\eta_{n}
is null-homologous.
Take
h_{n}
a sequence of orbit equivalences between
\psi^{n}
and
\phi
, so that
h_{n}
converge toward the identity map. According to
Lemma
56
,
the measure
\Theta_{h_{n}}(\eta_{n})
converges toward
\nu_{\mathfrak{p}}
. So for
n
large enough, the minimum of
\link_{\phi}(\Theta_{h_{n}}(\eta_{n}),\nu)
with all
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{p}^{0}(\phi)
, is positive
(see Corollary
17
).
It follows from
Lemma
18

that the measure
\eta_{n}
satisfies the Reeb-like property.
∎

5

On the linking number for invariant measures

In this section, we prove the theorems and lemmas stated in
Section
1.5
.
Fix one transitive Anosov flow
\phi
on a closed oriented 3-dimensional manifold
M
. Except in the proof of Theorem
15
,
we also suppose that the stable and unstable foliations of
\phi
are orientable. We define the linking number between two null-homologous invariant signed measures starting for two signed measures
\nu,\mu
of the type
\Leb_{\gamma}
, then allowing
\nu
to be any null-homologous invariant signed measure, and then for all
\nu,\mu
. To distinguish the several definitions, we use the notations
\link_{\phi}^{ff}
,
\link_{\phi}^{f}
and respectively
\link_{\phi}
(
f
for finitely supported). We prove that they coincide on their common domains.

Let
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
be two algebraic multi-orbits, supposed to be null-homologous and with disjoint supports. We define the following linking number

\link_{\phi}^{ff}(\Leb_{\Gamma_{1}},\Leb_{\Gamma_{2}})=S_{1}\mathrlap{\hskip 2.3pt\cdot}{\cap}\Gamma_{2}

where
S_{1}
is any 2-chain bounded by
\Gamma_{1}
. It does not depend on the choice of
S_{1}
.

The proof of Theorem
15

follows two steps. First consider a null-homologous algebraic multi-orbit
\Gamma
and a signed measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\phi)
. We define the linking number
\link_{\phi}^{f}(\Leb_{\Gamma},\mu)
roughly as the integral of the transverse measure
\mu^{\perp}
on a 2-chain
S
bounding
\Gamma
. The linking number
\link_{\phi}^{f}(\Leb_{\Gamma},\mu)
is well-defined for all
\mu
, even when
\mu
charges the support of
\Leb_{\Gamma}
.
The linking number maps
\link_{\phi}^{ff}
and
\link_{\phi}^{f}
coincide where they are simultaneously defined.

In a second part, we use Markov partitions to give a precise description of the linking number. This part requires much more efforts and technical arguments. We summarize the strategy in Section
5.2
.

5.1

Linking number with
\Leb_{\Gamma}

Fix a null-homologous algebraic multi-orbit
\Gamma
. We define the linking number between
\Leb_{\Gamma}
and any
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\phi)
.

Take a set
\Delta\subset M
, obtained as a union of periodic orbits, containing the support of
\Gamma
. We consider the
blow-up

M_{\Delta}
of
M
along each orbit of
\Delta
, constructed below.

Denote by
X=\frac{\partial\phi_{t}}{\partial t}
the vector field generating
\phi
. Given a periodic orbit
\gamma
in
\Delta
, we denote by
N_{\gamma}
the normal vector bundle to
\gamma
. It is obtained as the set of equivalence classes of vectors
v
based on a point
p
in
\gamma
, non-parallel to
X(p)
, up to the equivalence relation
v_{1}\simeq v_{2}
if there exist
a>0,b\in\mathbb{R}
such that
v_{1}=av_{2}+bX(p)
. The set
M_{\Delta}
is the union of
M\setminus\Delta
and of the circle bundle
\cup_{\gamma}N_{\gamma}
, equipped with the atlas induced by
M\setminus\Delta
and by the angular sectors around
\Delta
.
We define the map
\pi_{\Delta}\colon M_{\Delta}\to M
by
\pi_{\Delta}(x)=x
for
x\in M\setminus\Delta
, and
\pi_{\Delta}(v)=x
if
v
is a vector based on
x\in\Delta
.

For a periodic orbit
\gamma\subset\Delta
, we denote by
\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}=\pi_{\Delta}^{-1}(\gamma)\subset M_{\Delta}
the boundary component corresponding to
\gamma
. Since
M
is supposed orientable,
\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}
is a torus. If the flow
\phi
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{k}
,
k\geq 1
, it lifts to a
\mathcal{C}^{k-1}
flow
\phi^{\Delta}
on
M_{\Delta}
, by setting
\phi_{t}^{\Delta}(v)=d\phi_{t}(v)
for a vector
v
based on a point in
\Delta
. We denote by
