X_{\Delta}=\frac{\partial\phi^{\Delta}_{t}}{\partial t}
the vector fields generating
\phi^{\Delta}
.

Notice that on each torus
\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}
, the restriction flow
\phi^{\Delta}_{|\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}}
has one or two periodic orbits corresponding to the stable direction, and one or two corresponding to the unstable direction. We refer to them as the stable and unstable orbits of
\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}
. Every other orbit converges in the past toward one stable orbit, and in the future toward one unstable orbit. From this remark follows the following lemma.

Lemma 59
.

For any signed measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\phi^{\Delta})
, the signed measure
\mu_{|\mathbb{T}_{\delta}}
is supported on the stable and unstable orbits of
\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}
.

For any signed measure
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\phi)
, there exists a signed measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\phi^{\Delta})
so that
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}^{*}\mu=\nu
and whose total variation is the same as the total variation of
\nu
.

Below, all homology and cohomology modules are taken with real coefficients, except when specified. We write
\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\phi^{\Delta})=(\pi_{\Delta}^{*})^{-1}\big(\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\phi)\big)
, that is the set of signed measures
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\phi^{\Delta})
satisfying
{[{\pi_{\Delta}^{*}\mu}]_{\phi}}=0
. Denote by

H^{1}(M_{\Delta})\xrightarrow{P_{\Delta}}H_{2}(M_{\Delta},\partial M_{\Delta})\xrightarrow{\partial}H_{1}(\partial M_{\Delta})

the Lefschetz-Poincaré duality and the boundary map.

Lemma 60
.

For any signed measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\phi^{\Delta})
and
\omega\in H^{1}(M_{\Delta})
, the quantity
{[{\mu}]_{\phi^{\Delta}}}\cdot\omega
depends only on
\mu
and on
(\pi_{\Delta})_{*}(\partial P_{\Delta}(\omega))\in H_{1}(\Delta)
.

Proof.
Consider the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(M_{\Delta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{2}(M_{\Delta},\partial M_{\Delta})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(\partial M_{\Delta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{2}(M,\Delta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(\Delta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{\Delta})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{\Delta})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\Delta}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

Where
P
is the Poincaré duality map and the gray rectangle is a sub-sequence of the long exact sequence in homology, coming from the inclusion
\Delta\subset M
. It is clear that the right part is commutative.
The cohomology set
H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z})
is naturally isomorphic to the set of homotopy class of maps of the form
f\colon M\to\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. For a smooth map
f\colon M\to\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
,
P
can be described by
P([f^{*}dt])=[f^{-1}(x)]
for any regular value
x
of
f
. A similar description holds for
P_{\Delta}
. Using these characterizations and the fact that
H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z})
spans
H^{1}(M,\mathbb{R})
, one can verify that the left part of the diagram also commutes.

Let
\omega\in H^{1}(M_{\Delta})
be a 1-form which satisfies
(\pi_{\Delta})_{*}(\partial P_{\Delta}(\omega))=0
. By exactness, there exists
S\in H_{2}(M)
satisfying
j^{*}S=(\pi_{\Delta})_{*}(P_{\Delta}(\omega))
. By commutativity, we have
\omega=\pi_{\Delta}^{*}\circ P^{-1}(S)
. It follows that

{[{\mu}]_{\phi^{\Delta}}}\cdot\omega={[{\pi_{\Delta}^{*}\mu}]_{\phi}}\cdot P^{-1}(S)=0

∎

Given
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\phi^{\Delta})
satisfying
{[{\pi_{\Delta}^{*}\mu}]_{\phi}}=0
, we define
\link_{\Gamma}^{\Delta}(\mu)={[{\mu}]_{\phi^{\Delta}}}\cdot[\alpha]
for any smooth differential 1-form
\alpha
with
(\pi_{\Delta})_{*}(\partial P_{\Delta}(\omega))=[\Gamma]\in H_{1}(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})
. Notice that
(\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\phi^{\Delta}))\mapsto\link_{\Gamma}^{\Delta}(\mu)
is continuous.

Lemma 61
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\phi^{\Delta})
be an invariant signed measure,
\gamma
be a periodic orbit in
\Delta
and
\alpha
be a closed 1-form on
\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}
.
Then the integral
\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}}\iota_{X_{\Delta}}\alpha d\mu
depends only on
\mu(\mathbb{T}_{\gamma})
and on
[\alpha]\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}_{\gamma},\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.
Thanks to
Lemma
8
,
the integral
\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}}\iota_{X_{\Delta}}\alpha d\mu
does not depend on the choice of the closed 1-form
\alpha
in its cohomology class.
Denote by
dt
the closed 1-form on
\gamma
satisfying
\iota_{X}dt=1
. We denote by
\beta^{\perp}=\pi_{\Delta}^{*}dt
and by
\beta^{\parallel}
a closed 1-form which is not null-cohomologous and such that the stable and unstable periodic orbits on
\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}
are tangent to
\ker(\beta^{\parallel})
. Then
\iota_{X_{\Delta}}\beta^{\parallel}
is zero on the support of
\mu_{|\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}}
(see Lemma
59
), so one has

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}}\iota_{X_{\Delta}}\beta^{\parallel}d\mu

\displaystyle=0\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}}\iota_{X_{\Delta}}\beta^{\perp}d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}}\pi_{\Delta}^{*}(\iota_{X}dt)d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\gamma}}1d\mu

\displaystyle=\mu(\mathbb{T}_{\gamma})

The lemma follows by linearity and from the fact that
[\beta^{\perp}]
and
[\beta^{\parallel}]
form a basis of
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}_{\gamma},\mathbb{R})
.
∎

Lemma 62
.

Given a null-homologous algebraic multi-orbit
\Gamma
and a signed measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\phi^{\Delta})
, the quantity
\link_{\Gamma}^{\Delta}(\mu)
depends only on
\Gamma
and
\pi_{\Delta}^{*}(\mu)\in\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\phi)
. In particular, it does not depend on
\Delta
.

Proof.
The previous lemma implies that
\link_{\Gamma}^{\Delta}(\mu)
depends on
\pi_{\Delta}^{*}\mu
and not on
\mu
itself. To prove that it does not depend on
\Delta
, take
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
two choices of finite unions of periodic orbits containing the support of
\Gamma
. By taking
\omega_{1}\in H^{1}(M_{\Delta_{1}},\mathbb{R})
as in the definition of
\link_{\Gamma}^{\Delta_{1}}(\mu)
, and pushing it back to an element in
H^{1}(M_{\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2}},\mathbb{R})
it follows that we have

\link_{\Gamma}^{\Delta_{1}}(\mu)=\link_{\Gamma}^{\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2}}(\mu)=\link_{\Gamma}^{\Delta_{2}}(\mu)

∎

Definition 63
.

For a null-homologous algebraic multi-orbit
\Gamma
in
M
, and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\phi)
, we define
\link_{\phi}^{f}(\Leb_{\Gamma},\mu)
as
\link_{\Gamma}^{\Delta}(\nu)
for any union of periodic orbits
\Delta
containing the support of
\Gamma
, and any signed measure
\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\phi^{\Delta})
satisfying
\pi_{\Delta}^{*}\nu=\mu
.

Proposition 64
.

Let
\Delta
be a finite union of periodic orbits. The map
\link_{\phi}^{f}
is continuous on
\{\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\phi),\support(\mu)\subset\Delta\}\times\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\phi)
.
Additionally, we have

\link_{\phi}^{f}(\Leb_{\Gamma_{1}},\Leb_{\Gamma_{2}})=\link_{\phi}^{ff}(\Leb_{\Gamma_{1}},\Leb_{\Gamma_{2}})

for any null-homologous algebraic multi-orbit
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
with disjoint support.

Remark 65
.

A consequence of Banach-Steinhaus Theorem is the following. Given a sequence of signed measures
\mu_{n}
converging toward a signed measure
\mu_{\infty}
, the total variation of
\mu_{n}
is bounded by some constant independent on
n
.

Proof.
Take a sequence
(\nu_{n},\mu_{n})_{n}
in
\{\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\phi),\support(\mu)\subset\Delta\}\times\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\phi)
, converging toward
(\nu_{\infty},\mu_{\infty})
. Denote by
\Gamma_{n}
and
\Gamma_{\infty}
the algebraic multi-orbit corresponding to
\nu_{n}
and
\nu_{\infty}
. As explained in
Remark
65
,
the total variations of
\nu_{n}
and
\mu_{n}
are bounded by some
C>0
.

We take a sequence
(\mu_{\Delta,n})_{n}
in
\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\phi^{\Delta})
so that
\pi_{\Delta}^{*}\mu_{\Delta,n}=\mu_{n}
and the total variation of
\mu_{\Delta,n}
is at most
C
. We can decompose
\mu_{\Delta,n}
as a difference of two invariant positive measures
\mu_{\Delta,n}^{+},\mu_{\Delta,n}^{-}
, so that each of them as total mass at most
C
. By compactness, the sequence
(\mu_{\Delta,n}^{+},\mu_{\Delta,n}^{-})_{n}
accumulates toward a pair of positive measures
(\mu_{\Delta,\infty}^{+},\mu_{\Delta,\infty}^{-})
, which satisfies
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}^{*}(\mu_{\Delta,\infty}^{+}-\mu_{\Delta,\infty}^{-})=\mu_{\infty}
. So we have
\link_{\phi}^{f}(\nu_{\infty},\mu_{\infty})=\link_{\Gamma_{\infty}}^{\Delta}(\mu_{\Delta,\infty}^{+})-\link_{\Gamma_{\infty}}^{\Delta}(\mu_{\Delta,\infty}^{-})
.

Since
\Delta
is a finite union of periodic orbits, there exists a continuous map
\Gamma\mapsto\omega_{\Gamma}
, so that
\omega_{\Gamma}
is in
H^{1}(M_{\Delta},\mathbb{R})
and satisfies
(\pi_{\Delta})^{*}(\partial P_{\Delta}(\omega_{\Gamma}))=[\Gamma]\in H_{1}(\Delta,\mathbb{R})
. The quantities
\link_{\Gamma_{n}}^{\Delta}(\mu_{\Delta,n}^{\pm})={[{\mu_{\Delta,n}^{\pm}}]_{\phi^{\Delta}}}\cdot\omega_{\Gamma_{n}}
converges toward
\link_{\Gamma_{\infty}}^{\Delta}(\mu_{\Delta,\infty}^{\pm})
. It follows that
\link_{\phi}^{f}(\nu_{n},\mu_{n})
converge toward
\link_{\phi}^{f}(\nu_{\infty},\mu_{\infty})
.

The last point is a consequence of Poincaré duality. Take two algebraic multi-orbits
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
, an algebraic multi-orbit
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{2}=\sum_{i}m_{i}\gamma_{i}
in
M_{\Delta}
with
\pi_{*}(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{2})=\Gamma_{2}
. Take a closed 1-form
\alpha
on
M_{\Delta}
and a 2-chain
S
satisfying
[S]=P_{\Delta}[\alpha]
and
(\pi_{\Delta})_{*}(\partial S)=\Gamma_{1}
, then we have:

\displaystyle\int_{M_{\Delta}}\iota_{X_{\Delta}}\alpha d\Leb_{\widetilde{\Gamma}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}m_{i}\int_{\gamma_{i}}\alpha

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}m_{i}\gamma_{i}\mathrlap{\hskip 2.3pt\cdot}{\cap}S\qquad\text{by duality}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{2}\mathrlap{\hskip 2.3pt\cdot}{\cap}(\pi_{\Delta})_{*}(S)

\displaystyle=\link_{\phi}^{ff}(\Leb_{\Gamma_{1}},\Leb_{\Gamma_{2}})

∎

As a consequence, one obtains the self-linking number of a null-homologous algebraic multi-orbit as the linking number between a Seifert surface of the algebraic multi-orbit and the stable direction, which is taken as a definition of the self-linking number by
Ghys
[
Ghy09
]
.

5.2

Linking function for a Markov partition

Suppose that
\phi
is an Anosov flow whose stable and unstable foliations are orientable.
To prove
Theorem
15
,
we transfer the problem to a question on invariant signed measures for a finite sub-shift using a Markov partition
\mathcal{P}
. Markov partitions makes the linking number computable with precise formulas. Lets us describe the strategy.

Given an invariant signed measure
\mu
on
\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
, we associate to
\mu
a signed measure
\mathcal{N}(\mu)
supported on finitely many periodic orbits, so that the linking number with
\mu-\mathcal{N}(\mu)
can be computed as an integral. To see that, start with a periodic orbit
\gamma
of the flow which intersects exactly twice a given rectangle
{\mathcal{R}}
. As described in Section
3.1
, there exists a transverse pair of pants bounded by
\gamma
and by two shorter periodic orbits
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
. The pair of pants can be seen as a 4-gon
P
inside the rectangle
{\mathcal{R}}
glued to two strips tangent to the flow (
P
is not unique and depends on some choices). The linking number between
\gamma-\delta_{1}-\delta_{2}
and another null-homologous algebraic multi-orbit
\Gamma
(with disjoint support) is equal to the algebraic intersection between
\Gamma
and
P
(assuming that
\partial P
is disjoint from the multi-orbit). Similarly, the linking number between
\gamma-\delta_{1}-\delta_{2}
and any invariant signed measure
\nu
is equal to

\link_{\phi}^{f}(\Leb_{\gamma}-\Leb_{\delta_{1}}-\Leb_{\delta_{2}},\nu)=\pm\int_{P}\nu^{\perp}

where the sign depends on if
P
is positively oriented or not (it depends on the relative position of
\gamma
and
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
). A small combinatorial trick can be used to replace
P
in the integral by a sub-rectangle
{\mathcal{R}}_{\gamma}
of
{\mathcal{R}}
which depends on
\gamma,\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
and on no more choices.

Denote by
u
a periodic
\mathcal{P}
-word whose realization is
\gamma
and by
\eta_{u}
the
\sigma
-invariant signed measure supported by the orbit of
u
. There exists a map
\mathfrak{L}\colon\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}^{2}\to\{-1,0,1\}
which satisfies that
|\mathfrak{L}(u,v)|=1
if and only if
v\in{\mathcal{R}}_{\gamma}
. Additionally the linking number can be computed as follows

\link_{\phi}^{f}(\Leb_{\gamma}-\Leb_{\delta_{1}}-\Leb_{\delta_{2}},\nu)=\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}^{2}}\mathfrak{L}(x,y)d\eta_{u}\oplus\nu^{\perp}

Replace
\Leb_{\gamma}
by a signed measure supported on finitely many orbits. A similar equation holds (where
\delta_{i}
has to be replaced by a suitable collection of periodic orbits). We describe in Section
5.3
a general construction. Given a
\sigma
-measure
\mu
(playing the role of
\Leb_{\gamma}
), we define another
\sigma
-invariant signed measure
\mathcal{N}(\mu)
supported on finitely many orbits (playing the role of
\sum_{i}\Leb_{\delta_{i}}
) for which we can apply the above formula. We actually use the equation to define the linking number
\link_{\phi}(\mu-\mathcal{N}(\mu),\nu)
as the integral
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}^{2}}\mathfrak{L}(x,y)d\mu\oplus\nu^{\perp}
. This integral is proven to be continuous in
(\mu,\nu)
.

The signed measure
\mathcal{N}(\mu)
is supported on the set of orbits which intersects the rectangle
{\mathcal{R}}
at most once (in some sense), so we view
\mathcal{N}
as a reduction map. Applying it successively for all rectangles of the Markov partition yields a reduction map whose image is the set of signed measures supported by the (finite set of) periodic orbits which intersect all rectangles at most once. The set of normalized signed measures is finite dimensional, so the linking number for these signed measures is well defined (measures supported on finitely many orbits) and is continuous. One defines the linking number
\link_{\phi}(\mu,\nu)
as the sum
\link_{\phi}(\mu-\mathcal{N}(\mu),\nu)+\link_{\phi}(\mathcal{N}(\mu),\nu)
, which is continuous in
(\mu,\nu)
.

We fix a Markov partition
\mathcal{P}
for the flow
\phi
. Denote by
({\mathcal{R}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{R}}_{p})
the family of Markov rectangles in
\mathcal{P}
. We fix two orientations on the stable and unstable foliations, and consider the induced vertical and horizontal orders on
\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
.

Recall that we denote by
\Theta_{\mathcal{P}}\colon\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{M}_{s}(\phi)
the map sending
\sigma
-invariant signed measures to the corresponding
\phi
-invariant signed measures. For
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
, we denote by
{[{\mu}]_{\phi}}={[{\Theta_{\mathcal{P}}(\mu)}]_{\phi}}\in H_{1}(M,\mathbb{R})
the homology class corresponding to
\mu
.
We denote by
\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\sigma)
the set of signed measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
satisfying
{[{\mu}]_{\phi}}=0
.

Definition 66
.

For two signed measures
\nu,\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\sigma)
supported by finitely many periodic orbits, we define the linking number of
\nu
and
\mu
by

\link_{\sigma}^{ff}(\nu,\mu)=\link_{\phi}^{f}(\Theta_{\mathcal{P}}(\nu),\Theta_{\mathcal{P}}(\mu))

Measure zero sets.

Because of the Portmanteau Lemma, it is useful to identify some sets which have zero measures for all signed measures in
\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
. A set
X\subset\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
is said to have
uniformly zero measure
if for all
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
, one has
|\mu|(X)=0
. Notice that it is enough to satisfy the property on probability measures.

Lemma 67
(Portmanteau Lemma for almost continuous functions).

Let
X
be a metrizable topological space endowed with the Borel algebra. Let
Y\subset X
be a measurable set and
f\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
a bounded function, which is continuous on
X\setminus Y
. Given a sequence
(\mu_{k})_{k}
of signed measures on
X
, weakly converging toward a signed measure
\nu
, one has

\uplim_{k\to+\infty}\bigg|\int_{X}fd\mu_{k}-\int_{X}fd\nu\bigg|\leq 2|\nu|(Y)\cdot\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}

A particular case is when
|\nu|(Y)=0
, where we have convergence of the integrals of
f
. This lemma is adapted from a standard Portmanteau Lemma where the function is required to be continuous everywhere (see
[
Kle08
, Theorem 13.16]
for the usual Portmanteau Lemma).

Proof.
Take a distance
d
compatible with the topology on
X
. For
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, denote by
h_{t}^{+}\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
the map defined by
h_{t}^{+}(x)=\sup_{y\in X}(f(y)-td(x,y))
. The map
h_{t}^{+}
is well-defined since
f
is bounded, and
t
-Lipschitz as supremum of
t
-Lipschitz maps. Notice that we have
f\leq h_{t}\leq\sup f\leq\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}
for all
t
. It follows that

\uplim_{k}\int_{X}fd\mu_{k}\leq\uplim_{k}\int_{X}h_{t}^{+}d\mu_{k}=\int_{X}h_{t}^{+}d\nu

For any
x
in
X\setminus Y
,
f
is continuous at
x
so when
t
goes to
+\infty
,
h_{t}^{+}(x)
is decreasing and converges toward
f(x)
. Hence for all
k
one has

\displaystyle\uplim_{t\to+\infty}\int_{X}h_{t}^{+}d\nu

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\big(\uplim_{t}h_{t}^{+}\big)d\nu\qquad\text{according to Fatou's Lemma}

\displaystyle=\int_{Y}\big(\uplim_{t}h_{t}^{+}\big)d\nu+\int_{X\setminus Y}\big(\uplim_{t}h_{t}^{+}\big)d\nu

\displaystyle\leq|\nu|(Y)\cdot\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}+\int_{X\setminus Y}fd\nu

\displaystyle\leq|\nu|(Y)\cdot\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}+\int_{X}fd\nu-\int_{Y}fd\nu

\displaystyle\leq\int_{X}fd\nu+2|\nu|(Y)\cdot\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}

It follows that

\uplim_{k}\int_{X}fd\mu_{k}-\int_{X}fd\nu\leq 2|\nu|(Y)\cdot\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}

Using the map
h_{t}^{-}(x)=\inf_{y}(f(y)+td(x,y))
, the same arguments prove that

-\lowlim_{k}\bigg(\int_{X}fd\mu_{k}-\int_{X}fd\nu\bigg)\leq 2|\nu|(Y)\cdot\lVert f\rVert_{\infty}

∎

We now prove two lemmas stating that two families of sets have uniformly zero measures. Given a rectangle
{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathcal{P}
, define the sets:

E_{\mathcal{R}}^{n+}=\{u\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}},u_{n}={\mathcal{R}},\text{ and for all }k>n,u_{k}\neq{\mathcal{R}}\}

and

E_{\mathcal{R}}^{n-}=\{u\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}},u_{n}={\mathcal{R}},\text{ and for all }k<n,u_{k}\neq{\mathcal{R}}\}

We also define
E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}=\cup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(E_{\mathcal{R}}^{n-}\cup E_{\mathcal{R}}^{n+})
, the set of
\mathcal{P}
-word containing a
{\mathcal{R}}
at some index, and admitting a half sub-word without
{\mathcal{R}}
.

Lemma 68
.

The set
E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}
has uniformly zero measure.

Proof.
Take a signed measure
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
.
By definition, we have
\sigma^{m}(E_{\mathcal{R}}^{n+})=E_{\mathcal{R}}^{(n+m)+}
and
E_{\mathcal{R}}^{n+}\cap E_{\mathcal{R}}^{m+}=\emptyset
when
n\neq m
. Since
\nu
is
\sigma
-invariant, one has
\nu(\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}})\geq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\nu(E_{\mathcal{R}}^{n+})\geq\sum_{n}\nu(E_{\mathcal{R}}^{0+})
. The signed measure
\nu
is finite so
\nu(E_{\mathcal{R}}^{n+})=0
for all
n
. Similarly
\nu(E_{\mathcal{R}}^{n-})=0
and
\nu(E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm})=0
.
∎

A second family of sets with uniformly zero measures are the union of stable and unstable leaves, after removing the periodic points. Take a point
v\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
. Its strong stable manifold
W^{s}(v)
is the set of point
w
such that
d(\sigma^{n}(v),\sigma^{n}(w))\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}0
. Notice that
W^{s}(v)
is the set of
\mathcal{P}
-words which coincide with
v
for large enough indexes.

Lemma 69
.

Let
w^{s}\subset\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
be a strong stable leaf. If
w^{s}
contains a periodic point
u
, then it has a unique periodic point
u
and
w^{s}\setminus\{u\}
has uniformly zero measure. Otherwise
w^{s}
has uniformly zero measure.

Proof.
Suppose that
u,v\in w^{s}
are periodic. Denote by
n
a common multiple of the periods of
u
and
v
. Then the distance between
\sigma^{kn}(u)=u
and
\sigma^{kn}(v)=v
goes to zero when
k
goes to
+\infty
. Hence we have
u=v
.

Suppose that
w^{s}
contains a periodic point
u
, of period
N\geq 1
. Denote by
w^{s}_{n}=\{v\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}},v_{n-1}\neq u_{n-1},\forall k\geq n,v_{k}=u_{k}\}
, so that
w^{s}\setminus\{u\}=\cup_{n}w^{s}_{n}
. The sets
w^{s}_{n}
are pairwise disjoints and
\sigma^{N}(w^{s}_{n})=w^{s}_{n-N}
. So using the same argument as in the previous proof, the sets
w^{s}_{n-N}(v)
have uniformly zero measures. Then
w^{s}\setminus\{u\}
has uniformly zero measure.

Suppose now that
w^{s}
contains no periodic point. If there would exist two distinct integers
n<m\in\mathbb{Z}
and an element
u
in
\sigma^{n}(w^{s})\cap\sigma^{m}(w^{s})
, then
u
would be
(m-n)
-periodic after a certain index. So the limit
\lim_{k}\sigma^{k(m-n)+n}(u)
would exist and be in
w^{s}
. It is not possible so for any
n\neq m
, we have
\sigma^{n}(w^{s})\cap\sigma^{m}(w^{s})=\emptyset
. Then the previous argument shows that
w^{s}
has uniformly zero measure.
∎

5.3

Reduction to finitely many orbits

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a Markov partition and fix a rectangle
{\mathcal{R}}\in{\mathcal{R}}
. We define a reduction map, which takes an orbit
\gamma
of the flow which intersects
{\mathcal{R}}
, cuts it along its intersection with
{\mathcal{R}}
, and transforms (using a Shadowing Lemma) each orbit arc into a periodic orbit intersecting
{\mathcal{R}}
exactly once. The precise definition is written below. It is somehow the opposite operation to the one we use in Section
3.1
: we build a Fried section by merging two periodic orbits into one.

Then we push back a measure by this reduction map. Given an invariant measure
\mu
, it yields another invariant measure
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mu)
that is supported on orbits which intersects
{\mathcal{R}}
at most once (in some sense). Doing this successively on all rectangles of the Markov partition yields a measure
\mathcal{N}(\mu)
which is supported on finitely many periodic orbit. We relate in the later section the reduction map with the linking number.

Fix a rectangle
{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathcal{P}
.
We call
{\mathcal{R}}
-reduction map
the map
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}\colon\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}\to\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
defined as follows. For
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
with
n\leq m
, we denote
S_{n,m}=\{u\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}},u_{n}={\mathcal{R}}=u_{m},{\mathcal{R}}\not\in u_{{\llbracket n+1,m-1\rrbracket}}\}
. Then we define:

\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}(u)=\begin{cases}\overline{u_{|{\llbracket n,m-1\rrbracket}}}&\text{ if\penalty\ $u$ belongs to\penalty\ $S_{n,m}$ for some\penalty\ $n\leq 0$ and\penalty\ $m\geq 1$}\\
u&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

In the first case,
\overline{u_{|{\llbracket n,m-1\rrbracket}}}
is the unique periodic
\mathcal{P}
-word of period
m-n
, which coincides with
u
on the set of indexes
{\llbracket n,m\rrbracket}
. Notice that for all
u\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
,
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}(u)
coincides with
u
for the indexes
0
and
1
.
After giving a formula for
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\mu
for finitely supported signed measures
\mu
, we prove the following. The map
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}
induces a continuous map
\mathcal{M}_{s}(\phi)\to\mathcal{M}_{s}(\phi)
, which additionally preserves the homology class of signed measures.

Let
u
be a cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-word. Denote by
\eta_{u}\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
the measure
\sum_{k=0}^{|u|-1}\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{k}(\overline{u})}
, which is the unique invariant measure supported on the orbit of
\overline{u}
, normalized by
\eta_{u}(\overline{u})=1
.

Lemma 70
.

Let
u
be a cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-word with
u(0)={\mathcal{R}}
. Denote by
v_{1}\ldots v_{n}
the unique family of cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-word which satisfies
v_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{n}=u
and
v_{j}^{-1}({\mathcal{R}})=\{0\}
for all
j
. Then we have

\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\eta_{u}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{v_{j}}

Proof.
If
k\in{\llbracket 0,|u|-1\rrbracket}
satisfies
\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}|v_{i}|\leq k<\sum{i=1}^{j}|v_{i}|
for some
j\in{\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}
, then we have
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}\circ\sigma^{k}(\overline{u})=\sigma^{k-\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}|v_{i}|}(\overline{v}_{j})
. It follows that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{i}(\overline{u})}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}\circ\sigma^{k}(\overline{u})}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{k-\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}|v_{i}|}(\overline{v}_{j})}

Hence we have
\sum_{k=0}^{|u|-1}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{k}(\overline{u})}=\sum_{j}\sum_{k=0}^{|v_{j}|-1}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{k}(\overline{v}_{j})}=\sum_{j}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\eta_{v_{j}}
.
∎

Lemma 71
.

The map
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}\colon\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}\to\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
is continuous on
\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}\setminus E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}
, and is measurable.

Proof.
The topology on
\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
is generated by the family of open sets of the form
C_{v}=\{u\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}},u_{|{\llbracket-m,m\rrbracket}}=v\}
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and for
\mathcal{P}
-words
v\colon{\llbracket-m,m\rrbracket}\to\mathcal{P}
. We determine the preimages of these sets by
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}
.

When the letter
{\mathcal{R}}
does not appear inside
v
, we have
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(C_{v})=C_{v}
. Suppose that
v
contains the letter
{\mathcal{R}}
in position
n_{1}\leq 0
and
n_{2}\geq 1
. We take
n_{1}
maximal and
n_{2}
minimal with these properties. When
v
is the restriction to
[-m,m]
of the periodic
\mathcal{P}
-word of period
v_{|{\llbracket n_{1},n_{2}-1\rrbracket}}
, then we have
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(C_{v})=C_{v_{|{\llbracket n_{1},n_{2}\rrbracket}}}
. When
v
is not such a restriction, we have
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(C_{v})=\emptyset
.

Lastly suppose that
v
contains the letter
{\mathcal{R}}
in position
n\leq 0
, but it does not contain this letter on positive positions. The last case is similar to this one. We can take
n\leq 0
maximal with
v_{n}={\mathcal{R}}
. For
u\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
, the image
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}(u)
is in
C_{v}
if and only if one of the two following cases are satisfied:

•

u
contains no
{\mathcal{R}}
on positive positions, and
u_{|{\llbracket-m,m\rrbracket}}=v
. The set of such word
u
is a closed subset of
\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
, and is contained inside
E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}
.

•

For some
k>m
,
u
contains a
{\mathcal{R}}
on position
k
, contains no
{\mathcal{R}}
on positions
[1,k-1]
,
u_{|{\llbracket-m,m\rrbracket}}=v
and
u_{|{\llbracket k-n-m,k\rrbracket}}
coincides with
v_{|{\llbracket-m,n\rrbracket}}
. For
k
fixed, the set of such words
u
is open.

Hence in any case,
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(C_{v})
is a union of open sets and of one closed set contained in
E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}
. So it is measurable and continuous outside
E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}
.
∎

Lemma 72
.

For all
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
,the pushed forward signed measure
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\mu
is invariant by
\sigma
.

Proof.
We prove that
\mu((\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}\circ\sigma)^{-1}(A))=\mu((\sigma\circ\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}(A))
by considering the intersection between
(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}\circ\sigma)^{-1}(A)
and two subspaces of
\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
, which are
S_{1,1}=\{u\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}},u_{1}={\mathcal{R}}\}
and its complement.

For any element
u
in
\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}\setminus S_{1,1}
, we have
\sigma\circ\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}(u)=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}\circ\sigma(u)
. Indeed, when
u
contains no coefficient equal to
{\mathcal{R}}
for all positive indexes or for all non-negative indexes, then
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}(u)=u
and
\sigma(u)
satisfies the same condition. When there exist
n\leq 0
maximal and
m\geq 2
minimal such that
u_{n}={\mathcal{R}}=u_{m}
, it follows from the definition that
\sigma\circ\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}(u)=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}\circ\sigma(u)
. Hence

\mu((\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}\circ\sigma)^{-1}(A)\setminus S_{1,1})=\mu((\sigma\circ\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}(A)\setminus S_{1,1})

Consider a
\mathcal{P}
-words
u\in S_{1,1}
with
u_{1}={\mathcal{R}}
. Denote by
E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}=\cup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(E_{\mathcal{R}}^{n+}\cup E_{\mathcal{R}}^{n-})
the set of
\mathcal{P}
-words which contain the letter
{\mathcal{R}}
somewhere and admit an infinite half sub-word without
{\mathcal{R}}
. According to Lemma
68
, we have
\mu(E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm})=0
. Notice that
S_{1,1}\setminus E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}=(\sqcup_{n\leq 0}(S_{n,1}))\setminus E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}=\sqcup_{m\geq 2}(S_{1,m})\setminus E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}
. For
n\leq 0
, we define the homeomorphism
\tilde{g}_{n}\colon S_{n,1}\mapsto S_{1,2-n}
by
\tilde{g}_{n}(u)=\sigma^{-n-1}(u)
. Notice that
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}\circ\sigma(\tilde{g}_{n}(u))=\sigma\circ\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}(u)
, so
\tilde{g}_{n}
restricts to a homeomorphism
g_{n}\colon(\sigma\circ\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}(A)\cap S_{n,1}\setminus E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}\mapsto(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}\circ\sigma)^{-1}\cap S_{1,2-n}\setminus E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}
. It follows that

\displaystyle\mu\big((\sigma\circ\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}(A)\cap S_{1,1}\big)

\displaystyle=\mu\big((\sigma\circ\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}(A)\cap(S_{1,1}\setminus E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm})\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\leq 0}\mu\big((\sigma\circ\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}(A)\cap(S_{n,1})\setminus E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\leq 0}\mu\big(\sigma^{-n-1}\big((\sigma\circ\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}})^{-1}(A)\cap(S_{n,1})\setminus E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}\big)\big)

since
\mu
is
\sigma
-invariant

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\leq 0}\mu\big((\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}\circ\sigma)^{-1}(A)\cap(S_{1,2-n})\setminus E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}\big)

\displaystyle=\mu\big((\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}\circ\sigma)^{-1}(A)\cap S_{1,1}\setminus E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}\big)

\displaystyle=\mu\big((\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}\circ\sigma)^{-1}(A)\cap S_{1,1}\big)

Hence one has
\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\mu)(A)=\mu(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}\circ\sigma^{-1}(A))=\mu(\sigma^{-1}\circ\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(A))=\mu(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(A))=(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\mu)(A)
, concluding.
∎

Proposition 73
.

The map
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\colon\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
is continuous.

Proof.
Consider a sequence of signed measures
\mu_{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
weakly converging toward the signed measure
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
. Take a continuous map
g\colon\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathbb{R}
. The map
g\circ\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}\colon\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathbb{R}
is bounded, measurable and continuous outside the set
E_{\mathcal{R}}^{\pm}
which has uniformly zero measure. According to the Portmanteau Lemma
67
, the sequence of integrals
\int gd(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\mu_{k})=\int g\circ\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}d\mu_{k}
converges toward
\int gd(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\nu)
, which proves the continuity of
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}
.
∎

Proposition 74
.

For every signed measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
, we have
{[{\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\mu}]_{\phi}}={[{\mu}]_{\phi}}
. Additionally we have
|\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\mu|(\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}})\leq|\mu|(\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}})
.

The statement on the total variation is clear. For
x,y\in\mathcal{P}
, denote by
A_{x,y}
the set
\{v\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}},v_{0}=x,v_{1}=y\}
.
Notice that for all
u\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
,
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}(u)
and
u
have the same coefficients at the indexes
0
and
1
. Hence for
\nu=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\mu-\mu
, we have
\nu(A_{x,y})=0
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{P}
. The proposition follows from the next lemma.

Lemma 75
.

For any signed measure
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
satisfying
\nu(A_{x,y})=0
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{P}
, we have
{[{\nu}]_{\phi}}=0
.

Proof.
Take a smooth closed 1-form
\alpha
on
M
. For each Markov rectangle
{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathcal{P}
and its corresponding cuboid
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{R}}
, we denote by
f_{\mathcal{R}}\colon{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{R}}\to\mathbb{R}
a smooth function whose differential coincides with
\alpha
. We denote by
i_{\mathcal{R}}\colon{\mathcal{R}}\to\partial^{+}{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{R}}
the map obtained by pushing
{\mathcal{R}}
along the flow.

Recall that
M_{\mathcal{P}}
is the suspension set over
\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
and
h_{\mathcal{P}}\colon M_{\mathcal{P}}\to M
is the corresponding semi-conjugation. Denote by
T\colon\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
the suspension time and by

M_{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathcal{R}}=\{(x,t)\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}\times\mathbb{R},0\leq t<T(x)\}

the subset of
M_{\mathcal{P}}
corresponding to the cuboid
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{R}}
(minus the boundary
\partial^{+}{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{R}}
). We have

\displaystyle\int_{M}\iota_{X}\alpha d\Theta(\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{M_{\mathcal{P}}}(\iota_{X}\alpha)\circ h_{\mathcal{P}}d(\nu\otimes\Leb)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathcal{P}}\int_{M_{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathcal{R}}}(df_{\mathcal{R}}(X))\circ h_{\mathcal{P}}d(\nu\otimes\Leb)

Denote by
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
the set of
u\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
satisfying
u_{0}={\mathcal{R}}
. Using the local product of
M_{\mathcal{P}}
one proves

\int_{M_{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathcal{R}}}(df_{\mathcal{R}}(X))\circ h_{\mathcal{P}}d(\nu\otimes\Leb)=\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}}(f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ i_{\mathcal{R}}-f_{\mathcal{R}})\circ h_{\mathcal{P}}(x,0)d\nu(x)

Denote by
f^{+},f^{-}\colon\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathbb{R}
the maps whose restrictions to
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
are given respectively by
f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ i_{\mathcal{R}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}
and
f_{\mathcal{R}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}
. Then:

\displaystyle\int_{M}\iota_{X}\alpha d\Theta(\nu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathcal{P}}\int_{\Sigma_{{\mathcal{R}}}}(f_{\mathcal{R}}^{+}-f_{\mathcal{R}}^{-})d\nu

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}}(f^{+}-f^{-})d\nu

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}}(f^{+}-f^{-}\circ\sigma)d\nu

Notice that in a neighborhood of a point
u\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
,
f^{+}
and
f^{-}\circ\sigma
are obtained from integrating
\alpha
on the Markov sub-rectangle given by
\partial^{+}{\mathcal{C}}_{u_{0}}\cap u_{1}
. It follows that
f^{+}-f^{-}\circ\sigma
is constant on each set
A_{x,y}
. Therefore the integral
\int_{A_{x,y}}(f^{+}-f^{-}\circ\sigma)d\nu
is zero. Since the non empty sets of the form
A_{x,y}
give a partition of
\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
, we have
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}}(f^{+}-f^{-}\circ\sigma)d\nu
. It follows that
\int_{M}\iota_{X}\alpha d\Theta(\nu)=0
for all
\alpha
, and that
{[{\nu}]_{\phi}}=0
.
∎

Denote by
({\mathcal{R}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{R}}_{p})
the Markov rectangles in the Markov partition
\mathcal{P}
. Following Fried’s terminology
[
Fri82
]
, we call
minimal loop
any periodic orbits of
\sigma
which intersects every rectangle at most once. That is
u\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
is a minimal loop if it is periodic of period some
T\geq 1
, and for any
{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathcal{P}
there is at most one
i\in{\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket}
such that
u_{i}={\mathcal{R}}
. Denote by
\prim
the set of minimal loops and notice that it has a finite cardinal.

Consider the product map
\prod_{i=1}^{p}\mathcal{N}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{{i}}}\colon\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}\to\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
(taken in any order).

Lemma 76
.

For any
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
, the support of
\big(\prod_{i=1}^{p}\mathcal{N}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{{i}}}\big)^{*}\mu
is included inside
\prim
.

Proof.
For a signed measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
, denote by
\support(\mu)\subset\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
the support of
\mu
.
First notice that for any
i
and
u\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
,
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{{i}}}(u)
is either equal to
u
or is a minimal loop. It follows that given a signed measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
, we have
\support(\mathcal{N}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{{i}}}^{*}\mu)\subset\support(\mu)\cup\prim
. Hence

\support\Bigg(\Bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{N}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{{i}}}\Bigg)^{*}\mu\Bigg)\cup\prim

is a decreasing sequence in
k
.

Recall that
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}=\{u\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}},u_{0}={\mathcal{R}}\}
. Denote by
A_{\mathcal{R}}
the set of points whose orbit intersects
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
in a single
\mathcal{P}
-word. That is either it takes the value
{\mathcal{R}}
at most once, or it is periodic and its fundamental domain contains at most one
{\mathcal{R}}
. Notice that
A_{\mathcal{R}}
is closed. For any
u
in
\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
, the element
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}(u)
satisfies one of the following properties:

1.

\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}(u)
is in
A_{\mathcal{R}}
, when
u
is in
A_{\mathcal{R}}
or when we have
u_{n}={\mathcal{R}}=u_{m}
for some
n\leq 0,m\geq 1
,

2.

\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}(u)
takes the value
{\mathcal{R}}
on exactly one of the two intervals
{\rrbracket-\infty,0\rrbracket}
,
{\llbracket 1,+\infty\llbracket}
, when
u
satisfies the same property (and then
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}(u)=u
).

The set of
u
in the second case has uniformly zero measure according to Lemma
68
. So the support
\support(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\mu)
is included in
A_{\mathcal{R}}
. It follows from the above discussion that the support of
\big(\prod_{i=1}^{p}\mathcal{N}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{{i}}}\big)^{*}\mu
is included in
\cap_{\mathcal{R}}A_{\mathcal{R}}\cup\prim
.

It remains to see
\cap_{\mathcal{R}}A_{\mathcal{R}}=\prim
. It is clear that
\prim
is included in all
A_{\mathcal{R}}
and so in their intersection. Reciprocally a
\mathcal{P}
-word
u\in\cap_{\mathcal{R}}A_{\mathcal{R}}
is periodic (it cannot contains at most once every rectangle of
\mathcal{P}
, so it is in the other case for all
{\mathcal{R}}
). It follows that its fundamental domain contains at most once every rectangle of the Markov partition
u\in\prim
.
∎

Density of finitely supported measures.

For hyperbolic dynamical systems, density of measures supported on finitely many periodic orbits is a well known fact. We prove the density result for measures in a given homology classes (here for transitive symbolic dynamical systems and in Lemma
14
for transitive Anosov flows).

Lemma 77
.

Given
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
, there exists a sequence of signed measures
(\nu_{n})_{n}
in
\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
, converging toward
\nu
, so that for all
n
we have:

•

\nu_{n}
is finitely supported,

•

{[{\nu_{n}}]_{\phi}}={[{\nu}]_{\phi}}
,

•

|\nu_{n}|(\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}})\leq|\nu|(\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}})
.

Additionally if
\nu
is positive,
\nu_{n}
can be taken positive.

Proof.
For
n\geq 1
, we denote by
\mathcal{P}_{n}
the set of
\mathcal{P}
-words of the form
u\colon{\llbracket-n,n\rrbracket}\to\mathcal{P}
. An element
u\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
corresponds to a sub-rectangle of
u_{-n}
, consisting of points admitting a short itinerary along
\sigma^{-n}(u)
. Then
\mathcal{P}_{n}
corresponds to the Markov partition of
\phi
given by these rectangles.

Denote by
\Sigma_{n}
the set of bi-infinite
\mathcal{P}_{n}
-words, and
\sigma_{n}
the shift map on
\Sigma_{n}
. We have a natural map
\pi_{n}\colon\Sigma_{n}\to\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
, so that for
v\in\Sigma_{n}
,
\pi_{n}(v)
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
(\pi_{n}(v))_{i}=(v_{i})_{0}
. Notice that
\pi_{n}
is continuous bijective, so
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma_{n})\to\pi_{n}^{*}\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
is continuous and bijective.

For
n
and
{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, we denote by
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{n}\colon\Sigma_{n}\to\Sigma_{n}
the
{\mathcal{R}}
-reduction map for the Markov partition
\mathcal{P}_{n}
of
\phi
.
Given
n
, we fix an order on
\mathcal{P}_{n}
and denote by
\mathcal{N}_{n}
the product of the maps
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{{i}}}^{n}
for all
{\mathcal{R}}_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Here the product is ordered using the previously fixed order.

We claim that the sequence
\pi_{n}\circ\mathcal{N}_{n}\circ\pi_{n}^{-1}
converges uniformly toward the identity.
Notice that for any
u\in\Sigma_{n}
, we have
\mathcal{N}_{n}(u)_{0}=u_{0}
. It follows that for
v\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
,
\pi_{n}\circ\mathcal{N}_{n}\circ\pi_{n}^{-1}(v)
coincides with
v
on
{\llbracket-n,n\rrbracket}
. Hence
\pi_{n}\circ\mathcal{N}_{n}\circ\pi_{n}^{-1}(v)
converges uniformly toward
v
.

Take
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
and define the invariant signed measure
\nu_{n}=(\pi_{n}\circ\mathcal{N}_{n}\circ\pi_{n}^{-1})^{*}\nu
. From the claim follows that the sequence
\nu_{n}
converges toward
\nu
. The second and third points follow from
Proposition
74
.
∎

Lemma 78
.

Given a signed measure
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
and a finite set
\Delta\subset\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
, there exists a sequence of signed measures
(\nu_{n})_{n}
converging toward
\nu
, so that for all
n
:

•

\nu_{n}
has a finite support which is disjoint from
\Delta
,

•

{[{\nu_{n}}]_{\phi}}={[{\nu}]_{\phi}}
.

Proof.
Given a cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-word
w
, denote by
\eta_{w}
the invariant measure
\sum_{i=0}^{|w|-1}\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{i}(\overline{w})}
. Thanks to Lemma
77
, it is enough to prove the lemma when we have
\nu=\eta_{v}
for some cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-word
v
. We fix a cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-word
u
containing all rectangles of
\mathcal{P}
, so that
u_{0}=v_{0}
. We additionally suppose that
u
and
v
are not power of a common cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-word. The lemma follows from the two claims:

Claim 1
.

The sequence
(\frac{1}{k}\eta_{v^{k}u})_{k\geq 0}
converges weakly toward the measure
\eta_{v}
.

Claim 2
.

The signed measure
\frac{1}{k}(\eta_{v^{k}u}-\eta_{u})
is homologous to
\eta_{v}
.

Proof of Claim 1..

A large proportion of the words
\sigma^{i}(\overline{v^{k}u})
are close to the words of the form
\sigma^{j}(\overline{v})
when
k
is large. More precisely, the word
\sigma^{|v|n+m}(\overline{v^{k}u})
coincides with the word
\sigma^{m}(\overline{v})
on the indexes
{\llbracket-|v|n,|v|(k-n-1)\rrbracket}
, for
0\leq n<k
and
0\leq m\leq|v|
. It follows that with
m
fixed and for any sequence for which
n
and
k-n
goes to infinity,
\sigma^{|v|n+m}(\overline{v^{k}u})
converges toward
\sigma^{m}(\overline{v})
. Decompose the measure
\eta_{v^{k}u}
as a sum of three terms as follows

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\eta_{v^{k}u}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=0}^{k|v|+|u|}\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{i}(\overline{v^{k}u})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=0}^{\sqrt{k}-1}\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{i}(\overline{v^{k}u})}+\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=\sqrt{k}}^{k|v|+|u|-\sqrt{k}}\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{i}(\overline{v^{k}u})}+\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=k|v|+|u|-\sqrt{k}+1}^{k|v|+|u|}\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{i}(\overline{v^{k}u})}

The second term converges toward
\eta_{v}
. The total mass of the first and third terms are
\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}
, so these terms converge toward zero.
∎

Proof of Claim 2..

According to Lemma
70
, we have
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}(\eta_{v^{k}u}-\eta_{u})=k\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\eta_{v}
, and the map
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}
preserves the homology according to Proposition
74
.
∎∎

5.4

Linking function

Recall the notation
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}=\{u\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}},u_{0}={\mathcal{R}}\}
. In this section, we construct a measurable function
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}\colon\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}\times\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}\to\mathbb{R}
. Its key feature (proven in the next section) is that for all signed measures
\nu,\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
supported on finitely many orbits, one has
\link_{\sigma}(\nu-\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\nu,\mu)=\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}}\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}d\nu\otimes\mu
. We prove another important property: the continuity of
\link_{\sigma}(\nu-\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\nu,\mu)
in
(\nu,\mu)
.

Denote by
\primR
the set of cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-word
u
with
u_{0}={\mathcal{R}}
and
u_{i}\neq{\mathcal{R}}
for all
i\neq 0
. For
u\in\primR
, We denote by
\Sigma_{u}
the set of
w\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
which satisfy
w_{k}=u_{k}
for all
k\in{\llbracket 0,|u|\rrbracket}
. Notice that for
w\in\Sigma_{u}
, the element
\sigma^{|u|}(w)
is in
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
. For
u\in\primR
, we define the two following measurable sets:

\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}=\{(w,x)\in\Sigma_{u}\times\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}},\overline{u}<_{h}x\text{ and }\sigma^{|u|}(w)\leq_{v}x<_{v}w\}

\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{-}=\{(w,x)\in\Sigma_{u}\times\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}},\overline{u}<_{h}x\text{ and }w\leq_{v}x<_{v}\sigma^{|u|}(w)\}

Figure 6:
Illustration of
(w,x)\in\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{-}
and
(w^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}
.

The definitions of the sets
\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{\pm}
can seem coming out of nowhere, but they are justified by a geometric picture in the next section. One may think about the Fried section in Section
3.1
, obtained as the union of a transverse 4-gon and two tangent strips. The sets
\mathfrak{R}^{\pm}_{u}
play the role of the 4-gon, which we can use to compute linking number.

We call
linking function
the function
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}\colon\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}=\sum_{u\in\primR}\big(\mathds{1}_{\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}}-\mathds{1}_{\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{-}}\big)

Here
\mathds{1}_{X}
is the characteristic map of the set
X
. From the definitions follows the next lemma.

Lemma 79
.

For any
u\in\primR
, the following holds:

1.

For any
(w,x)\in\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}
, we have
w<_{v}\overline{u}
,

2.

For any
(w,x)\in\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{-}
, we have
w>_{v}\overline{u}
,

3.

\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}\cap\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{-}=\emptyset
,

4.

\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}
is measurable,

5.

\lVert\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}\rVert_{\infty}=1
.

The next theorem and proposition are critical to define the linking number between two invariant signed measures.

Theorem 80
.

For
\nu,\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
supported by finitely many
periodic orbits and satisfying
{[{\nu}]_{\phi}}={[{\mu}]_{\phi}}=0
, we have

\link_{\sigma}^{ff}(\nu-\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\nu,\mu)=\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}}\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}d\nu\otimes\mu

The proof of the theorem is postponed to next subsection.

Proposition 81
.

The map
\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)\times\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)\to\mathbb{R}
given by
(\nu,\mu)\mapsto\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}}\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}d(\nu\otimes\mu)
is continuous.

Let us sketch the proof of the proposition. The key idea is to use the Portmanteau Lemma on
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}
, so we would like to have that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}
is continuous outside a set of small measure.
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}
is the difference between the characteristic maps of two sets, so it is continuous outside the boundary of these sets. Let us look at these boundaries.

As suggested in Figure
6
, for
u
and
w\in\Sigma_{u}
fixed, the projections of
\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{-}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}
onto the
x\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
coordinate correspond to two sub-rectangles of
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
. The topology of
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
is so that the vertical boundaries on the right of the figure are not actually boundaries inside
\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
(the cylinders in
\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
are both closed and open, so they have no boundary). The vertical boundaries on the left are included in unstable leaves (minus the periodic point) so they have mass zero. The vertical boundaries are included in stable leaves, so they have mass zero outside the potential periodic points (
w
and
\sigma^{|u|}(w)
when
w
is periodic). When they contain periodic points, we cut in
\mathfrak{R}^{\pm}_{u}
a small rectangle around
w
and glue it to
\sigma^{|u|}(w)
(we use
\sigma^{|u|}
for the identification). Using the argument above, the boundary of the small rectangles have mass zero, and we have removed two points with positive mass. The mass of the sets remain the same since the measures are
\sigma
-invariant.

The general argument has to take into account two more difficulties. First
w
varies so the boundary are slightly more difficult to handle. Secondly
u
varies too. We prove that the mass is mostly concentrated on finitely many values of
u
. That is the union of
\mathfrak{R}^{\pm}_{u}
have small masses when taking all values of
u
excepts for finitely many ones. The ideas are precisely stated in the three following lemmas.

Lemma 82
(Estimation Lemma 1).

For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
A\in\mathbb{N}
, such that for all signed measures
\nu,\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
, we have:

|\nu\otimes\mu|\Bigg(\bigcup_{u\in\primR,|u|\geq A}\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}\Bigg)\leq\epsilon|\nu\otimes\mu|(\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}^{2})

For
u
in
\primR
and
k\geq 0
, we denote by
u^{k}
the concatenation of
k
copies of
u
, with
u^{0}={\mathcal{R}}
by convention.
We define the sets

\Sigma_{u,k}=\Sigma_{u^{k}}\setminus\Sigma_{u^{k+1}}

and

\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}=\{(w,x)\in\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+},w\in\Sigma_{u,k}\}

Recall that we denote
W^{s}_{l}(v)=\{w\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}},\text{ for all }k\geq 0,w_{k}=v_{k}\}
.
Notice that
\Sigma_{u}\setminus W^{s}_{l}(\overline{u})
is the union of
\Sigma_{u^{k}}\setminus\Sigma_{u^{k+1}}
for all
k\geq 1
. Given
w
in
W^{s}_{l}(\overline{u})
, we do not have
\sigma^{|u|}(w)<_{v}w
nor
\sigma^{|u|}(w)>_{v}w
, so no
(w,x)
can be in
\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}\cup\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{-}
. Therefore the sets
\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{-}
are the unions of the set
\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{-}
for all
k\geq 1
.

Lemma 83
(Estimation Lemma 2).

For all
\epsilon>0
and
u\in\primR
, there exists
B\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all signed measures
\nu,\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
, we have

|\nu\otimes\mu|\Bigg(\bigcup_{k\geq B}\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}\Bigg)\leq\epsilon|\nu\otimes\mu|(\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}^{2})

Lemma 84
(Estimation Lemma 3).

Given
u\in\primR
and
k\geq 1
, the quantity
(\nu\otimes\mu)(\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+})
is continuous in
(\nu,\mu)\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)\times\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
.

Proof of Proposition
81
.
Assume the three estimation lemmas.
Let
(\nu_{n},\mu_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of pairs of signed measures in
\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
, converging toward a pair of signed measures
(\nu_{\infty},\mu_{\infty})
. We should prove that
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}}\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}d(\nu_{n}\otimes\mu_{n})
converges toward
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}}\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}d(\nu_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{\infty})
.

According to Remark
65
,
the total variations of the signed measures
\nu_{n}\otimes\mu_{n}
are bounded by some
C>0
. Take
\epsilon>0
and
A\in\mathbb{N}
given by the first estimation lemma for
\epsilon
. Denote by
N\in\mathbb{N}
the number of elements
u\in\primR
with
|u|<A
. Take
B\in\mathbb{N}
the largest integer given by the second estimation lemma, applied for all
u
with
|u|<A
and for
\epsilon/N
. We have

\displaystyle\uplim_{n\to+\infty}\Bigg(\nu_{n}\otimes\mu_{n}\Bigg(\bigcup_{u\in\primR}\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\uplim_{n}\Bigg(\nu_{n}\otimes\mu_{n}\Bigg(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|u|<A,k<B\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}\Bigg)\Bigg.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\nu_{n}\otimes\mu_{n}\Bigg(\bigcup_{|u|\geq A}\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\Bigg.+\nu_{n}\otimes\mu_{n}\Bigg(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|u|<A,k\geq B\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\uplim_{n}\Bigg(\nu_{n}\otimes\mu_{n}\Bigg(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|u|<A,k<B\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+C\epsilon+N\cdot\frac{C\epsilon}{N}

\displaystyle\leq\nu_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{\infty}\Bigg(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|u|<A,k<B\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}\Bigg)+2C\epsilon

according to the estimation Lemma 3

\displaystyle\leq\nu_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{\infty}\Bigg(\bigcup_{u}\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}\Bigg)+4C\epsilon\text{ for all }\epsilon>0

\displaystyle\leq\nu_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{\infty}\Bigg(\bigcup_{u}\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\lowlim_{n}\Bigg(\nu_{n}\otimes\mu_{n}\Bigg(\bigcup_{u\in\primR}\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\nu_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{\infty}\Bigg(\bigcup_{u}\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}\Bigg)

Hence
(\nu_{n}\otimes\mu_{n})(\cup_{u}\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+})
converges toward
(\nu_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{\infty})(\cup_{u}\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+})
. The same statement holds for
\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{-}
. Therefore the integrals
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}}\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}d(\nu_{n}\otimes\mu_{n})
converge toward
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}}\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}d(\nu_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{\infty})
.
∎

Now we prove the estimation lemmas.

Proof of Lemma
82
.
Given
A\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
L_{A}
the union of the sets
\Sigma_{u}
for
u\in\primR
with
|u|\geq A
. Notice that if
w
is in
L_{A}
, then
w_{0}={\mathcal{R}}
and
w_{k}\neq{\mathcal{R}}
for all
k\in{\llbracket 1,A-1\rrbracket}
. Hence for two distinct indexes
i,j\in{\llbracket 0,A-1\rrbracket}
, the set
\sigma^{i}(L_{A})
and
\sigma^{j}(L_{A})
are disjoint. For a signed measure
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
, we have:

A|\nu|(L_{A})=\sum_{i=0}^{A-1}|\nu|(\sigma^{i}(L_{A}))\leq|\nu|(\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}})

so
|\nu|(L_{A})\leq\frac{|\nu|(\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}})}{A}
. We take
A
satisfying
1\leq A\epsilon
. Then we have:

|\nu\otimes\mu|\Bigg(\bigcup_{|u|\geq A}\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}\Bigg)\leq|\nu\otimes\mu|\big(L_{A}\times\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}\big)\leq\epsilon|\nu\otimes\mu|(\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}^{2})

∎

Proof of Lemma
83
.
Take
(w,x)
in
\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}
. Then both
w
and
\sigma^{|u|}(w)
are in
\Sigma_{u^{k-1}}\setminus\Sigma_{u^{k+1}}
. By definition, we have
\sigma^{|u|}(w)\leq_{v}x<_{v}w
, so we also have
x\in\Sigma_{u^{k-1}}
. According to Lemma
79
, we have
x<_{v}w<_{v}\overline{u}
. So if
x
is in
\Sigma_{u^{k+1}}
,
u
is in
\Sigma_{u^{k+1}}
, so
w
is also in
\Sigma_{u^{k+1}}
. Since
w
is not is this set,
x
lies to
\Sigma_{u^{k-1}}\setminus\Sigma_{u^{k+1}}
.
Take
B>1
. Notice that we have
\sigma^{|u|}(\Sigma_{u^{k}})\subset\Sigma_{u^{k-1}}
, so given
i
with
0\leq 2i<B-1
, one has:

(\id\times\sigma^{2i})(\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+})\subset(\Sigma_{u^{k}}\setminus\Sigma_{u^{k+1}})\times\big(\Sigma_{u^{k-1-2i}}\setminus\Sigma_{u^{k+1-2i}}\big)

It follows that the sets
(\id\times\sigma^{2i})(\cup_{k\geq B}\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+})
for
0\leq 2i<B-1
are pairwise disjoint. For any
\nu,\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
, we have
(\nu\otimes\mu)\big((\id\times\sigma^{2i})(\cup_{k\geq B}\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+})\big)=(\nu\otimes\mu)\big(\cup_{k\geq B}\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}\big)
, so using a similar argument as above, we have:

(\nu\otimes\mu)\big(\cup_{k\geq B}\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}\big)\leq\frac{|\nu\otimes\mu|(\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}^{2})}{\Big\lfloor\frac{B-1}{2}\Big\rfloor}

∎

Denote by
S_{k}=\big\{w\in\Sigma_{u,k},w<_{v}\overline{u},\overline{u}<_{h}w\big\}
for
k\geq 1
.

Lemma 85
.

We have
S_{k}<_{v}S_{k+1}<_{v}\overline{u}
for all
k\geq 1
.

Proof.
Take
w_{1}\in S_{k}
and
w_{2}\in S_{k+1}
. Suppose that
w_{2}\leq_{v}w_{1}
. So
w_{1}
lies vertically between
w_{1}
and
\overline{u}
, which start by
u^{k+1}
. By definition of the vertical order,
w_{1}
starts by
u^{k+1}
, which is not possible since
\Sigma_{u,k}=\Sigma_{u^{k}}\setminus\Sigma_{u^{k+1}}
.
∎

We prove the estimation Lemma 3:
\nu\otimes\mu(\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+})
is continuous in
(\nu,\mu)\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)^{2}
. The idea is to use the Portmanteau Lemma. The boundary of
\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}
can have a non-zero mass, so we need a first step before using the Portmanteau Lemma. For that we notice that a signed measure
\nu\otimes\mu
charges
\partial\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}
only on pairs
(z,z)
and
(z,\sigma^{|u|}(z))
, where
z
is periodic. We cut a neighborhood of
(z,z)
in
\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}
and glue it on
(z,\sigma^{|u|}(z))
, to remove these points from the boundary of
\partial\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}
. Then we use the Portmanteau Lemma.

Proof of Lemma
84
.
Let
(\nu_{n},\mu_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of pairs of signed measures in
\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
converging toward a pair of signed measure
(\nu_{\infty},\mu_{\infty})
. We use a Portmanteau Lemma to prove the convergence of the integral.
Take
\epsilon>0
and
k\geq 1
. We can take a subset
X_{\epsilon}\subset S_{k}
for which the sum of
|\nu_{\infty}|(\{x\})
for
x\in S_{k}\setminus X_{\epsilon}
atom of
|\nu_{\infty}|
, is less that
\epsilon
. That is

\sum_{x\in S_{k}\setminus X_{\epsilon}}|\nu_{\infty}|(\{x\})\leq\epsilon

For each
z\in X_{\epsilon}
, we take a small neighborhood
T_{z}\subset\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}
of
z
. We can take it such that the following are satisfied:

Claim 1
.

There exist neighborhoods
T_{z}
of the points
z\in X_{\epsilon}
satisfying the following:

1.

\partial T_{z}=\emptyset
,

2.

the sets
T_{z}
and the sets
\sigma^{|u|}(T_{z})
all
z\in X_{\epsilon}
are pairwise disjoint,

3.

\sigma^{|u|}(T_{z})<_{v}\sigma(T_{z})
,

4.

\sigma^{|u|}(T_{z})>_{h}\overline{u}
for all
z\in X_{\epsilon}
.

Proof of Claim 1..

We take
T_{z}=\{w\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}},\text{ for all }|k|\leq N,w_{k}=z_{k}\}
for some
N>0
. It is open and close, so it has no boundary. If we prove the points 2, 3 and 4 when replacing
T_{z}
by
z
, then points 2 to 4 will be satisfied when taking
N
large enough.
If
z
is in
X_{\epsilon}\subset S_{k}
, then
\sigma^{|u|}(z)
is not in
\Sigma_{u^{k}}
, so it is not in
S_{k}
. Hence the set
X_{\epsilon}
and
\sigma^{|u|}(X_{\epsilon})
are pairwise disjoint.
Since
z
is in
\Sigma_{u}
and
z<_{v}\overline{u}
, we have
\sigma^{|u|}(z)<_{v}z
according to Lemma
85
. For
z\in X_{\epsilon}
, we have
\overline{u}<_{h}z
, and
\overline{u}
and
z
coincide on
{\llbracket 0,|u|\rrbracket}
. Hence we have
\overline{u}<_{h}\sigma^{|u|}(z)
. Therefore the points 2 to 4 are satisfied for large
N
.
∎

We define the sets

T=\Bigg(\bigcup_{z\in X_{\epsilon}}T_{z}\times T_{z}\Bigg)\bigcap\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}

and

\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}=\big(\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}\setminus T\big)\bigcup\Big(\id\times\sigma^{|u|}\Big)(T)

Claim 2
.

\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}\setminus T
and
(\id\times\sigma^{|u|})(T)
are disjoint, so
\nu_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+})=\nu_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})

Proof of Claim 2..

When
(w,x)
is in
\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}
we have
\sigma^{|u|}(w)\leq_{v}x
. When
(w,x)
is in
(\id\times\sigma^{|u|})(T)
, we have
(w,\sigma^{-|u|}(x))
is in
T\subset\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}
, so
\sigma^{-|u|}(x)<_{v}w
and
x<_{v}\sigma^{|u|}(w)
.
∎

We now prove that the boundary of
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}\subset\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}^{2}
has small measure for
\nu_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{\infty}
. Notice that for
z
in
X_{\epsilon}
, the point
(z,z)
is in the interior of
T
, so it is not in the adherence of
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
.

Claim 3
.

The set
T_{z}\times\sigma^{|u|}(T_{z})
is included in
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
.

Assume the claim. Denote
x\equiv_{v}w
when we have
x\leq_{v}w\leq_{v}x
, that is
x\in W^{s}_{l}(w)
. The boundary
\partial\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
is contained in union of the three sets:

1.

\{(w,x)\in\Sigma_{u,k}\times\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}},x\equiv_{h}\overline{u},\overline{u}<_{h}x\}

2.

\{(w,x)\in(\Sigma_{u,k}\setminus X_{\epsilon})\times\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}},x\leq_{v}\overline{u},\overline{u}\leq_{h}x,x\equiv_{v}w\}

3.

\{(w,x)\in(\Sigma_{u,k}\setminus X_{\epsilon})\times\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}},x\leq_{v}\overline{u},\overline{u}\leq_{h}x,x\equiv_{v}\sigma^{|u|}(w)\}

The mass for
|\nu_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{\infty}|
of the first set is zero
(see Lemma
69
).
For the two other sets, we have:

\displaystyle|\nu_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{\infty}|

\displaystyle\big(\{(w,x)\in(\Sigma_{u,k}\setminus X_{\epsilon})\times\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}},x\leq_{v}\overline{u},\overline{u}\leq_{h}x,x\equiv_{v}w\}\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{w\in\Sigma_{u,k}\setminus X_{\epsilon}}\bigg(\int_{x\in W^{s}_{l}(w)}\mathds{1}_{x\leq_{v}\overline{u},\overline{u}\leq_{h}x}d|\mu_{\infty}|(x)\bigg)d|\nu_{\infty}|(w)

\displaystyle=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\Sigma_{u,k}\setminus X_{\epsilon}\\
w\text{ periodic}\end{subarray}}\bigg(\int_{x\in W^{s}_{l}(w)}\mathds{1}_{x\leq_{v}\overline{u},\overline{u}\leq_{h}x}d|\mu_{\infty}|(x)\bigg)d|\nu_{\infty}|(w)\text{ according to Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lemmaLeafZeroMeasure}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\Sigma_{u,k}\setminus X_{\epsilon}\\
w\text{ periodic}\end{subarray}}\mathds{1}_{w\leq_{v}\overline{u},\overline{u}\leq_{h}w}|\mu_{\infty}|(\{w\})d|\nu_{\infty}|(w)

\displaystyle=\int_{w\in S_{k}\setminus X_{\epsilon}}|\mu_{\infty}|(\{w\})d|\nu_{\infty}|(w)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon|\nu_{\infty}|(\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}})

A similar argument shows that the third sets has mass at most
\epsilon|\nu_{\infty}|(\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}})
.
It follows from the Portmanteau
Lemma
67

that we have

\uplim_{n}\big|\nu_{n}\otimes\mu_{n}(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})-\nu_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})\big|\leq 2\epsilon|\nu_{\infty}|(\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}})

This holds for all
\epsilon>0
, and
\nu\otimes\mu(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})=\nu\otimes\mu(\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+})
for all
\nu,\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
. Therefore
\nu_{n}\otimes\mu_{n}(\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+})
converges in
n
toward
\nu_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+})
.

Proof of Claim 3..

Recall that for
x\in\sigma^{|u|}(T_{z})
, we have
\overline{u}<_{h}x
.
If
(w,x)
is in
T_{z}\times\sigma^{|u|}(T_{z})
and satisfies
w\leq_{v}\sigma^{-|u|}(x)
, then we have
\sigma^{|u|}(w)\leq_{v}x<_{v}w
. The second equation is due to
\sigma^{|u|}(T_{z})<_{v}\sigma(T_{z})
. In that case,
(w,x)
lies in
\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}
and not in
T
, so
(w,x)\in\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
.

If
(w,x)\in T_{z}\times\sigma^{|u|}(T_{z})
satisfies
\sigma^{-|u|}(x)<_{v}w
, we also have
\sigma^{|u|}(w)\leq_{v}\sigma^{-|u|}(x)
. Indeed
\sigma^{|u|}(w)
belongs to
S_{k-1}
,
\sigma^{-|u|}(x)
belongs to
S_{k}
, and
S_{k}>_{v}S_{k-1}
according to Lemma
85
. Hence
(w,\sigma^{-|u|}(x))
is in both
\mathfrak{R}_{u,k}^{+}
and
T_{z}^{2}
, so it lies in
T
. Hence
(w,x)
is in
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
.
∎∎

5.5

Adapted cells

We prove Theorem
80
, that is the linking number
\link_{\sigma}^{ff}(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\nu-\nu,\mu)
is equal to
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}}\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}d\nu\otimes\mu
when
\mu
and
\nu
are finitely supported. For that, recall that the Fried sections
S
are constructed as the union of a 4-gon
P
in a Markov rectangle
{\mathcal{R}}
and two strips tangent to the flow. The linking number between
\partial S
and a null-homologous algebraic multi-orbit
\Gamma
is equal to (plus or minus) the algebraic intersection between
P
and
\Gamma
. Here we replace
P
with a sub-rectangle of
{\mathcal{R}}
which depends on no choices (contrary to the edges
P
which depend on arbitrary choices). We define an adapted 2-cell (an nice enough cell of
{\mathcal{R}}
) which generalize the 4-gon
P
and the sub-rectangle. It allows us to precisely compute the linking number.

Denote by
\Delta^{n}
the
n
-dimensional simplex. We call
n
-chains, the abstract finite sums of continuous maps
\Delta^{n}\to{\mathcal{R}}
, with coefficients in
\mathbb{R}
. We also denote by
\partial
the boundary map. A differential embedding
\Delta^{1}\to{\mathcal{R}}
is said adapted if it is either transverse to the stable and the unstable foliations, or is tangent to one of them. A 1-chain is said
adapted
if it is the sum of adapted embeddings
\Delta^{1}\to{\mathcal{R}}
. For
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and
f_{i}\colon\Delta^{2}\to{\mathcal{R}}
, the 2-chain
c=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}f_{i}
is said adapted if the maps
f_{i}
are orientation preserving immersions and
\partial f_{i}
are adapted. Here
\Delta^{2}
is endowed with the anti-clockwise orientation.

Recall that
W^{s}_{\mathcal{R}}
and
W^{u}_{\mathcal{R}}
are the stable and unstable foliations on
{\mathcal{R}}
.
We identify the bi-foliated rectangle
({\mathcal{R}},W^{s}_{\mathcal{R}},W^{u}_{\mathcal{R}})
with a sub rectangle of
(\mathbb{R}^{2},H,V)
, with a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
diffeomorphism preserving the orientations of the foliations.
Let
C
be an adapted 2-chain. We define the
support function
of
C
has
\Sup_{C}\colon{\mathcal{R}}\to\mathbb{R}
defined for
x\in{\mathcal{R}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

\Sup_{C}(x)=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}C\mathrlap{\hskip 2.3pt\cdot}{\cap}(x+(-t,t^{2}))

In other words when
C
is an immersed polygon (positively oriented),
\Sup_{C}(x)
is equal to 1 when one the following is true:

•

x
is in the interior of
C
,

•

x
is on a edge
e
of
C
which has a non-negative slope and
C
is adjacent to
e
by above,

•

x
is on a edge
e
of
C
which has a negative slope and
C
is adjacent to
e
by below,

•

x
is on a corner of
C
, and
C
contains an angular sector based at
x
and of angle
(\pi-\epsilon,\pi)
for some small
\epsilon>0
.

The value of
\Sup_{C}
on the boundary of
C
is made to be additive in
C
. Let
c
be an adapted 1-cycle. We define
\Sup_{c}=\Sup_{C}
for any 2-chain
C
satisfying
\partial C=c
.

Remark 86
.

Given an adapted 2-cell
C
, if either
\partial C=0
or
C
is contained in finitely many stable leaves and one unstable leave, then
\Sup_{C}=0
.

Reducing a cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-word

We fix a primitive cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-word
r
with
r_{0}={\mathcal{R}}
. Denote by
\eta_{r}
the measure
\eta_{r}=\sum_{k=0}^{|r|-1}\mathds{1}_{\sigma^{k}(\overline{r})}
. We construct an adapted 2-cell to prove the Theorem
80
for the measure
\eta_{r}
.
Write
r=s_{1}\circ\ldots\circ s_{n}
where all
s_{i}
are cyclic words such that
(s_{i})^{-1}({\mathcal{R}})=\{0\}
. According to Lemma
70
, we have
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\eta_{r}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\eta_{s_{j}}
.

We suppose that
n\geq 2
. For
i\in{\llbracket 0,n-1\rrbracket}
, we denote by
r_{i}=s_{i}\circ\ldots\circ s_{n}\circ s_{1}\circ\ldots\circ s_{i-1}
. Since
n>1
,
\overline{r}_{i}
and
\overline{s}_{i}
are different. So thanks to
Lemma
26
,
we can find a smooth curve
a_{i}\colon[0,1]\to{\mathcal{R}}
, from
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}\big(\overline{r_{i}}\big)
to
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}\big(\overline{s_{i}}\big)
, which is additionally transverse to the stable and unstable foliations.
Since the set of periodic orbits is countable, we can take the curve
a_{i}
such that its interior is disjoint from all period orbits.

Recall that when
x\in{\mathcal{R}}
admits an itinerary starting with
u_{0},u_{1},\cdots,u_{|u|}=u_{0}
, then
T^{u}(x)
is the time realizing this itinerary on
x
, and
\sigma^{u}(x)=\phi_{T^{u}(x)}(x)
.
Denote by
b_{i}\colon[0,1]\to{\mathcal{R}}
the curve given by
b_{i}(t)=\sigma^{s_{i}}\circ a_{i}(1-t)
, and define the adapted 2-chain
c=\sum_{i}(a_{i}+b_{i})
.

Lemma 87
.

For any cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-word
r
and any signed measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}^{0}_{s}(\sigma)
finitely supported, so that the support of
\Theta_{\mathcal{P}}(\mu)
is disjoint from the support of
\Theta_{\mathcal{P}}(\eta_{r})
, we have:

\link_{\sigma}^{ff}(\eta_{r}-\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\eta_{r},\mu)=\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}}\Sup_{c}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}d\mu

The map
\Sup_{c}
is used to build a 2-chain bounding the algebraic multi-orbit corresponding to
\eta_{r}-\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\eta_{r}
. It comes with a choice for the curves
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
. We remove later that choice by replacing
c
by another adapted 1-cycle. We denote by
v_{i}=a_{i}(0)=b_{i-1}(1)
,
w_{i}=a_{i}(1)=b_{i}(0)
.

Figure 7:
An adapted cell
c=\sum_{i}(a_{i}+b_{i})
and its support
\Sup_{c}
on the left. On the right is represented the corresponding 2-chain in
M
, bounded the algebraic multi-orbit.

Proof.
Given a periodic
\mathcal{P}
-word
u
, we denote by
\gamma_{u}\subset M
the algebraic multi-orbit corresponding to the periodic orbit of
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{u})
. According to
Lemma
25
,
the realization of
u
has degree one (here the foliations are orientable). Hence we have
\Theta_{\mathcal{P}}(\eta_{u})=\Leb_{\gamma_{u}}
. We also write
\Gamma=\gamma_{r}-\sum_{i}\gamma_{s_{i}}
.

The Lemma is clear when
r
does not contain
{\mathcal{R}}
, since
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\eta_{r}=\eta_{r}
in that case. Otherwise write
r=s_{1}\circ\ldots\circ s_{n}
as above. When
n=1
we also have
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\eta_{r}=\eta_{r}
.

We suppose now
n\geq 2
. Consider the curves
a_{i}
,
b_{i}
and
c=\sum_{i}(a_{i}+b_{i})
as above.
Denote by
C
an adapted 2-chain in
{\mathcal{R}}
, such that
\partial C=c
. We also denote by
e_{i}\subset M
the 2-chain obtained as the image of the map
[0,1]\times[0,1]\to M
,
(s,t)\mapsto\phi_{tT^{s_{i}}\circ a_{i}(s)}(a_{i}(s))
. Its boundary is
\partial e_{i}=a_{i}+\phi_{[0,T^{s_{i}}(w_{i})]}(w_{i})+b_{i}-\phi_{[0,T^{s_{i}}(v_{i})_{i}]}(v_{i})
.
Define a 2-chain by
S=\sum_{i}e_{i}-C
, illustrated in Figure
7
.

Claim 1
.

We have
\partial S=\Gamma
.

Take an algebraic multi-orbit
\Gamma^{\prime}=\sum_{\gamma}m(\gamma)\gamma
, whose support is disjoint from the support of
\Gamma
. By definition of the linking number
\link_{\phi}^{ff}
, we have
\link_{\phi}^{ff}(\Leb_{\Gamma},\Leb_{\Gamma^{\prime}})=S\mathrlap{\hskip 2.3pt\cdot}{\cap}\Gamma^{\prime}
. By construction of
a_{i}
,
e_{i}
is disjoint from
\Gamma^{\prime}
, so

\displaystyle\link_{\phi}^{ff}(\Leb_{\Gamma},\Leb_{\Gamma^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=C\mathrlap{\hskip 2.3pt\cdot}{\cap}\Gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma}m(\gamma)\sum_{x\in\gamma\cap{\mathcal{R}}}C\mathrlap{\hskip 2.3pt\cdot}{\cap}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma}m(\gamma)\sum_{x\in\gamma\cap{\mathcal{R}}}\Sup_{c}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{R}}}\Sup_{c}d\Leb_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}}\Sup_{c}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}d\mu

The last equality is due to the following. The measures
\Leb_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\perp}
and
\pi_{\mathcal{P}}^{*}\mu
coincide on the interior of
{\mathcal{R}}
. On the boundary of
{\mathcal{R}}
, the support
\Sup_{c}
is equal to zero, maybe outside finitely many points
w_{i}
and
v_{i}
. These points are in the support of
\eta_{r}
, so by hypothesis they are not in the support of
\mu
. Therefore we have
\int_{{\mathcal{R}}}\Sup_{c}d\pi_{\mathcal{P}}^{*}\mu=\int_{{\mathcal{R}}}\Sup_{c}\Leb_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\perp}
.

Proof of Claim 1..

Notice that
v_{i}=\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{r_{i}})
, so we have:

\displaystyle\phi_{T^{s_{i}}(v_{i})}(v_{i})

\displaystyle=\phi_{T^{s_{i}}}(\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{r_{i}}))

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\sigma^{|s_{i}|}(\overline{r_{i}}))

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{r_{i+1}})

\displaystyle=a_{i+1}(0)

Similarly we have
\phi_{T^{s_{i}}(w_{i})}(w_{i})=w_{i}
. By construction, we have:

\displaystyle\partial S

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\partial e_{i}-\partial C

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\big(a_{i}+b_{i}+\phi_{[0,T^{s_{i}}(w_{i})]}(w_{i})-\phi_{[0,T^{s_{i}}(v_{i})_{i}]}(v_{i})\big)-c

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\phi_{[0,T^{s_{i}}(v_{i})_{i}]}(v_{i})-\sum_{i}\phi_{[0,T^{s_{i}}(w_{i})]}(w_{i})

\displaystyle=\phi_{[0,T^{r}(v_{1})]}(v_{1})-\sum_{i}\phi_{[0,T^{s_{i}}(w_{i})]}(w_{i})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{r}-\sum_{i}\gamma_{s_{i}}

∎∎

We denote by
w_{i}^{-}=W^{s}_{\mathcal{R}}(v_{i})\cap W^{u}_{\mathcal{R}}(w_{i})
and
w_{i}^{+}=W^{s}_{\mathcal{R}}(v_{i+1})\cap W^{u}_{\mathcal{R}}(w_{i})
. We write
[a,b]_{s}
the stable segment in
{\mathcal{R}}
between two points
a,b
on a same stable leaf, and similarly for
[a,b]_{u}
. Denote by
\square c
the adapted 1-cycle given by
\sum_{i}([v_{i},w_{i}^{-}]_{s}+[w_{i}^{-},w_{i}^{+}]_{u}+[w_{i}^{+},v_{i+1}]_{s})
, which we call the square model of
c
(see Figure
8
).

Figure 8:
Adapted cell
c=\sum(a_{i}+b_{i})
and its corresponding square model
\square c
on the left. In dashed filling are two sets of equal areas, used in the proof of Lemma
88
. On the right is represented the set of points vertically between
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1}
, and on the right of
w_{i}
. These sets are used to relate the support function of
\square c
and the linking function
G_{\mathcal{R}}
, in the proof of Theorem
80
.

Lemma 88
.

For any
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
, we have
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}}(\Sup_{c}-\Sup_{\square c})\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}d\mu=0
.

Proof of Lemma
88
.
Denote
d_{i}=[v_{i},w_{i}^{-}]_{s}+[w_{i}^{-},w_{i}^{+}]_{u}+[w_{i}^{+},v_{i+1}]_{s}-a_{i}-b_{i}
, which is a closed adapted 1-chain.
We denote by
f_{i}\colon\Delta_{2}\to{{\mathcal{R}}}
an adapted 2-simplex whose boundary is
a_{i}+[w_{i},w_{i}^{-}]_{s}+[w_{i}^{-},v_{i}]_{s}
.
The image of
f_{i}
lies in the smallest horizontal rectangle of
{{\mathcal{R}}}
containing both
w_{i}=\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{s_{i}})
and
v_{i}=\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{r_{i}})
. Both
w_{i}
and
v_{i}
lie in
R_{s_{i}}
(the horizontal sub-rectangle of
{\mathcal{R}}
made of the points admitting a short itinerary along
s_{i}
, see Lemma
23
).
Hence the image of
f_{i}
is included in
{\mathcal{R}}_{s_{i}}
, so
\sigma^{s_{i}}\circ f_{i}
is well-defined and
\partial\sigma^{s_{i}}\circ f_{i}=-b_{i}+[w_{i},w_{i}^{+}]_{u}+[w_{i}^{+},v_{i+1}]_{s}
.

The 1-chain
\partial(\sigma^{s_{i}}\circ f_{i}-f_{i})-d_{i}
is equal to
[w_{i},w_{i}^{+}]_{u}-[w_{i},w_{i}^{-}]_{u}-[w_{i}^{-},w_{i}^{+}]_{u}
. According to Remark
86
, the support of the previous adapted 1-chain is zero. Therefore, we have
\Sup_{d_{i}}=\Sup_{\sigma^{s_{i}}\circ f_{i}}-\Sup_{f_{i}}
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
be an invariant signed measure. Since
\sigma^{s_{i}}\circ f_{i}
is obtained from
f_{i}
by pushing along the
\phi
, and the
\phi
preserves the orientations of the foliations
W^{s}_{\mathcal{R}},W^{u}_{\mathcal{R}}
, we have
\int\Sup_{\sigma^{s_{i}}\circ f_{i}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}d\mu=\int\Sup_{f_{i}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}d\mu
. It follows that
\int\Sup_{d_{i}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}d\mu=0
. Notice that we have
\sum_{i}d_{i}=\square c-c
, so the integral
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}}(\Sup_{c}-\Sup_{\square c})\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}d\mu
is equal to zero.
∎

We now prove that the integral
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}}\mathfrak{L}d(\nu\otimes\mu)
computes linking numbers.

Proof of Theorem
80
.
Notice that if
\nu(\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}})=0
, then
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\nu=\nu
and the two terms in the equality are zero. By linearity, it is enough to prove the result when
\nu
is equal to the measure
\eta_{r}
for some primitive cyclic
\mathcal{P}
-word
r
with
r_{0}={\mathcal{R}}
.
We use the notations
s_{i}
,
r_{i}
used in the above discussion.

Suppose that
n=1
. Then
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\eta_{r}=\eta_{r}
so
\link_{\sigma}^{ff}(\eta_{r}-\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\eta_{r},\mu)=0
.
Fix
u\in\primR
. If
u\neq r
, then
\eta_{r}\otimes\mu(\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{\pm})=0
since no points
w\in\Sigma_{u}
are in the support of
\eta_{r}
. If
u=r
, then we also have
\eta_{r}\otimes\mu(\mathfrak{R}_{r}^{\pm})=0
, since if
w
is in
\Sigma_{u}
and in the support of
\eta_{r}
, then
w=\overline{u}
and there is no
x\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
satisfying
w\leq_{v}x<_{v}\sigma^{|u|}(w)=w
or
\sigma^{|u|}(w)\leq_{v}x<_{v}w
. Hence we have
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{P}}^{2}}\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}d\eta_{r}\otimes\mu=0=\link_{\sigma}^{ff}(\eta_{r}-\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\eta_{r},\mu)
.

Now suppose that
n\geq 2
.
We denote by
I^{u}_{+}
the unstable boundary component of
{\mathcal{R}}
, which is on the right, and by
x_{i}=W^{s}(v_{i})\cap I^{u}_{+}
the projection of
v_{i}
on
I^{u}_{+}
, in parallel to
W^{s}_{\mathcal{R}}
. Define the adapted 2-cycle
f_{i}
by
f_{i}=[w_{i}^{-},w_{i}^{+}]_{u}+[w_{i}^{+},x_{i+1}]_{s}+[x_{i+1},x_{i}]_{u}+[x_{i},w_{i}^{-}]_{s}
.

Claim 1
.

We have
\sum_{i}\Sup_{f_{i}}=\Sup_{\square c}
(illustrated in Figure
8
).

Recall the definitions:

\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+}=\{(w,x)\in\Sigma_{u}\times\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}},\overline{u}<_{h}x\text{ and }\sigma^{|u|}(w)\leq_{v}x<_{v}w\}

\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{-}=\{(w,x)\in\Sigma_{u}\times\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}},\overline{u}<_{h}x\text{ and }w\leq_{v}x<_{v}\sigma^{|u|}(w)\}

Claim 2
.

For any
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
,
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}}\Sup_{f_{i}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}d\mu=(\mathds{1}_{\overline{r_{i}}}\otimes\mu)(\mathfrak{R}_{s_{i}}^{+})-(\mathds{1}_{\overline{r_{i}}}\otimes\mu)(\mathfrak{R}_{s_{i}}^{-})

Assume the two claims for now, and take
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\phi)
finitely supported, so that the support of
\Theta_{\mathcal{P}}(\mu)
is disjoint from the support of
\Theta_{\mathcal{P}}(\eta_{r})
.
Take
u\in\primR
.
Notice that
(\mathds{1}_{\overline{r_{i}}}\otimes\mu)(\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{\pm})=0
when
u\neq s_{i}
. It follows that:

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}}\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}d(\eta_{r}\otimes\mu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}}\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}d(\mathds{1}_{\overline{r_{i}}}\otimes\mu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\sum_{u\in\primR}\big((\mathds{1}_{\overline{r_{i}}}\otimes\mu)(\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{+})-(\mathds{1}_{\overline{r_{i}}}\otimes\mu)(\mathfrak{R}_{u}^{-})\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\big((\mathds{1}_{\overline{r_{i}}}\otimes\mu)(\mathfrak{R}_{s_{i}}^{+})-(\mathds{1}_{\overline{r_{i}}}\otimes\mu)(\mathfrak{R}_{s_{i}}^{-})\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}}\Sup_{f_{i}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}d\mu\qquad\text{ according to Claim 2.}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}}\Sup_{\square c}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}d\mu\qquad\text{ according to Claim 1.}

\displaystyle=\link_{\sigma}^{ff}(\eta_{r}-\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\eta_{r},\mu)\text{ according to Lemmas\penalty\ \ref{lemmaComputeLink},\penalty\ \ref{lemmaIndependentSurfaceModelEqual}}

Which implies
\link_{\sigma}^{ff}(\eta_{r}-\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\eta_{r},\mu)=\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}}\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{R}}d(\eta_{r}\otimes\mu)
.

Proof of Claim 1..

One can verify that the closed 1-chain
\sum_{i}f_{i}-\square c
is a chain contained in the union of the unstable leaf
I^{u}_{+}
and of finitely many stable leaves containing the points
v_{i}
. Essentially the parts in
[w_{i}^{-},w_{i}^{+}]_{u}
cancel each other. Hence its support function is
\Sup_{(\sum_{i}f_{i}-\square c)}=0
(see Remark
86
).
∎

Proof of Claim 2..

Notice that for
(w,x)\in\mathfrak{R}_{s_{i}}^{+}
to be in the support of
(\mathds{1}_{\overline{r_{i}}}\otimes\mu)
, we must have
w=\overline{r_{i}}
and
\overline{r_{i+1}}<_{v}\overline{r_{i}}
. Similarly for
(w,x)\in\mathfrak{R}_{s_{i}}^{-}
to be in the support of the same measure, we must have
\overline{r_{i}}<_{v}\overline{r_{i+1}}
. Hence the mass of either
\mathfrak{R}_{s_{i}}^{+}
or
\mathfrak{R}_{s_{i}}^{-}
is zero.

Consider the case when
\overline{r_{i+1}}<_{v}\overline{r_{i}}
.
By construction, we have
w_{i}^{+}<_{v}w_{i}^{-}
. So
f_{i}
is the boundary of a Markov rectangle
R_{i}\subset{\mathcal{R}}
with the induced orientation. Hence the support of the support of
f_{i}
is equal to:

\Sup_{f_{i}}(x)=\begin{array}[]{|ll}1&\text{if\penalty\ $x$ is in the interior of the rectangle\penalty\ $R_{i}$}\\
1&\text{ if }x\in(w_{i}^{+},x_{i+1}]_{s}\cup[x_{i+1},x_{i})_{u}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{array}

So the integral
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}}\Sup_{f_{i}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}d\mu
is equal to the mass for
\mu
of the subset of
\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}
of points
x
satisfying the two following conditions

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{s_{i}})=w_{i}

\displaystyle<_{h}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(x)

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{r_{i+1}})=v_{i+1}

\displaystyle\leq_{v}\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(x)<_{v}v_{i}=\pi_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{r_{i}})

According to
Lemma
26

this condition on
x
is equivalent to having

\displaystyle\overline{s_{i}}

\displaystyle<_{h}x

\displaystyle\sigma^{|s_{i}|}(\overline{r_{i+1}})

\displaystyle\leq_{v}x<_{v}\overline{r_{i}}

Hence we have
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}}\Sup_{f_{i}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}d\mu=\mu(\mathfrak{R}_{s_{i}}^{+})
. In the case
\overline{r_{i+1}}>_{v}\overline{r_{i}}
, the same argument shows that
\int_{\Sigma_{\mathcal{R}}}\Sup_{f_{i}}\circ\pi_{\mathcal{P}}d\mu=-\mu(\mathfrak{R}_{s_{i}}^{-})
.
∎∎

5.6

Linking number
\link_{\phi}

We compose the reduction maps for all Markov rectangles of
\mathcal{P}
to reduce an invariant signed measure on one supported by a fixed finite set. We then prove Theorem
15
. Denote by
({\mathcal{R}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathcal{R}}_{p})
the Markov rectangles in
\mathcal{P}
, by
\mathcal{N}_{{k}}=\mathcal{N}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{{k}}}\circ\ldots\circ\mathcal{N}_{{\mathcal{R}}_{{1}}}
and by
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{{p}}
.

Definition 89
.

For
\nu,\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{s}(\sigma)
such that
{[{\nu}]_{\phi}}={[{\mu}]_{\phi}}=0
, we define the
linking number
between
\nu
and
\mu
by
